\displaystyle{K_{a}(y)=i\sigma\int_{y}^{+\infty}e^{-\frac{1}{2}(u-\gamma)^{2}}\frac{\varphi_{a}(u)}{a+iu}du+c_{a}},c_{a}\,is\,constant

(3.49)

This representation has a sense.

(ii) From (3.43) and (3.45), we deduce that

\displaystyle{\varphi_{a}^{{}^{\prime}}(y)=i\sigma e^{\frac{1}{2}(y-\gamma)^{2}}\int_{y}^{+\infty}e^{-\frac{1}{2}(u-\gamma)^{2}}\frac{\varphi_{a}(u)}{a+iu}du+c_{a}e^{\frac{1}{2}(u-\gamma)^{2}}}

(3.50)

Theorem 3.16
.

(i) In the representation (3.45) we have
c_{a}=0

(ii)

\displaystyle{Lim\,e^{-\frac{1}{2}(y-\gamma)^{2}}\varphi_{a}^{{}^{\prime}}(y)=0}\,as\,y\longrightarrow-\infty\noindent(boundarycondition)

(3.51)

Proof

(i) By multiplying the two members of the equation (3.49) by
\displaystyle{e^{-\frac{1}{2}(u-\gamma)^{2}}}
, we get

\displaystyle{e^{-\frac{1}{2}(u-\gamma)^{2}}\varphi_{a}^{{}^{\prime}}(y)=i\sigma\int_{y}^{+\infty}e^{-\frac{1}{2}(u-\gamma)^{2}}\frac{\varphi_{a}(u)}{a+iu}du+c_{a}}

(3.52)

As the function
\displaystyle{u\longrightarrow e^{-\frac{1}{2}(u-\gamma)^{2}}\frac{\varphi_{a}(u)}{a+iu}}
is integrable we deduce that

\displaystyle{Lim\,e^{-\frac{1}{2}(y-\gamma)^{2}}\varphi_{a}^{{}^{\prime}}(y)=c_{a}}\,as\,y\longrightarrow-\infty

(3.53)

Now, if
c_{a}\neq 0
then we get that

\displaystyle{\varphi_{a}^{{}^{\prime}}(y)\thicksim c_{a}e^{\frac{1}{2}(y-\gamma)^{2}}}\,and\,\displaystyle{\frac{e^{-\frac{1}{2}y^{2}}}{y}\varphi_{a}^{{}^{\prime}}(y)\thicksim c_{a}\frac{e^{-\rho y}}{y}e^{\frac{\gamma^{2}}{2}-ia}}

(3.54)

We consider the space

\displaystyle{\mathbb{B}_{a}=\{\varphi_{a}:\mathbb{R}\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}entire;\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-\frac{1}{2}y^{2}}\mid\varphi_{a}(y)\mid^{2}dy<+\infty\}}

(3.55)

Then

(
\alpha
) From theorem 3.4, we deduce that
\forall\,\varphi\in\mathbb{B}
then
\varphi_{a}\in\mathbb{B}_{a}

(
\beta
) From lemma 3.2, we deduce that
\forall\,\varphi_{a}\in\mathbb{B}_{a}
then the function

\displaystyle{y\longrightarrow\frac{\varphi_{a}^{{}^{\prime}}(y)-\varphi_{a}^{{}^{\prime}}(0)}{y}\in\mathbb{B}_{a}}

As in (3.53) the function
\displaystyle{y\longrightarrow\frac{e^{-\rho y}}{y}e^{\frac{\gamma^{2}}{2}-ia}}
is not square integrable then
\varphi_{a}\notin\mathbb{B}_{a}

which is contradictory with the property (
\beta
) and therefore
c_{a}=0
.

(ii) we apply (i) to (3.53).
\square

Remark 3.17
.

By applying the above theorem, the last equation can be written as follows

\displaystyle{\varphi_{a}^{{}^{\prime}}(y)=i\sigma e^{\frac{1}{2}(y-\gamma)^{2}}\int_{-\infty}^{y}e^{-\frac{1}{2}(u-\gamma)^{2}}\frac{\varphi_{a}(u)}{a+iu}du;\,y\in\,]-\infty,0]}

(3.56)

(2) Justification for crossing to the limit in the equation (3.56) as
a\longrightarrow 0

Proposition 3.18
.

If
a\longrightarrow 0
in (3.56) then we get

\displaystyle{\varphi_{0}^{{}^{\prime}}(y)=\sigma e^{\frac{1}{2}(y-\rho)^{2}}\int_{-\infty}^{y}e^{-\frac{1}{2}(u-\rho)^{2}}\frac{\varphi_{0}(u)}{u}du;\,y\in\,]-\infty,0]}

(3.57)

Proof

As the functions
\displaystyle{a\longrightarrow\varphi_{a}^{{}^{\prime}}(y)}
and
\displaystyle{a\longrightarrow\frac{e^{-\frac{1}{2}(u-\rho-ia)^{2}}}{a+iu}\varphi_{a}(u)}
are continuous then

\displaystyle{\lim\limits_{a\rightarrow 0}\varphi_{a}^{{}^{\prime}}(y)=\varphi_{0}^{{}^{\prime}}(y)}\,and\,\displaystyle{\lim\limits_{a\rightarrow 0}\frac{e^{-\frac{1}{2}(u-\rho-ia)^{2}}}{a+iu}\varphi_{a}(u)=\frac{e^{-\frac{1}{2}(u-\rho)^{2}}}{iu}\varphi_{0}(u)}

(3.58)

We have to show that
\displaystyle{u\longrightarrow\frac{e^{-\frac{1}{2}(u-\rho)^{2}}}{iu}\varphi_{0}(u)}
is integrable. For this purpose we consider the following function

\displaystyle{f_{a}(u)=\frac{e^{-\frac{1}{2}(u-\rho-ia)^{2}}}{a+iu}\varphi_{a}(u)}

(3.59)

As for
\epsilon>0
enough small we have
\varphi_{a}(a+iu)\thicksim a+iu;u\in]-\epsilon,0]
, we deduce that

\displaystyle{\mid f_{a}(u)\mid\leq e^{\rho u}}

(3.60)

Now, for
u\in]-\infty,-\epsilon[
, we observe that

\displaystyle{\mid f_{a}(u)\mid\leq e^{-\frac{\rho^{2}}{2}}\frac{e^{\rho u}}{u}}

(3.61)

As the function

g(u)=\left\{\begin{array}[c]{l}\displaystyle{e^{-\frac{\rho^{2}}{2}}\frac{e^{\rho u}}{u}};u\in]-\infty,-\epsilon[\\
\\
\displaystyle{e^{\rho u}};u\in]-\epsilon,0]\\
\end{array}\right.

(3.62)

is integrable and the the sequence
f_{a}(u)
converges pointwise to
\displaystyle{\frac{e^{-\frac{1}{2}(u-\rho^{2}}}{iu}\varphi_{0}(u)}
as
a\longrightarrow 0
then by applying the Lebesgue’s Dominated Convergence Theorem, we deduce that

\displaystyle{\lim\limits_{a\rightarrow 0}\int_{-\infty}^{y}\frac{e^{-\frac{1}{2}(u-\rho-ia)^{2}}}{a+iu}\varphi_{a}(u)du=\int_{-\infty}^{y}\frac{e^{-\frac{1}{2}(u-\rho^{2}}}{iu}\varphi_{0}(u)}

(3.63)

Consequently, we get

\displaystyle{\varphi_{0}^{{}^{\prime}}(y)=\sigma e^{\frac{1}{2}(y-\rho)^{2}}\int_{-\infty}^{y}e^{-\frac{1}{2}(u-\rho)^{2}}\frac{\varphi_{0}(u)}{u}du;\,y\in\,]-\infty,0]}

(3.64)

.
\square

Theorem 3.19
.

(i) An operator integral associated to
H_{\mu,\lambda}
is given as follows

\displaystyle{K\psi(y)=\int_{-\infty}^{0}\mathcal{N}(y,s)\psi(s)ds}

(3.65)

where

(
\alpha
)
\rhd

\displaystyle{\psi(y)=\frac{\varphi_{0}^{{}^{\prime}}(y)}{y}e^{-\frac{y^{2}}{2}}\theta(y)}

(3.66)

with

\theta(y)=\left\{\begin{array}[c]{l}\,y;\quad y\in[-1,0]\\
\\
-1;\quad y\in]-\infty,-1]\\
\end{array}\right.

(
\beta
)
\rhd

\displaystyle{\mathcal{N}(y,s)=e^{-\rho y}\frac{\theta(y)}{y}.\frac{s}{\theta(s)}e^{\frac{s^{2}}{2}}\int_{-\infty}^{min(y,s)}e^{-\frac{1}{2}(u-\rho)^{2}}{u}du}

(3.67)

where

min(y,s)=\left\{\begin{array}[c]{l}y;\quad y\leq s\\
\\
s;\quad s\leq y\\
\end{array}\right.

(
\gamma
)
\rhd

\displaystyle{\psi\in L_{2}(]-\infty,0],\theta(y)dy)}

(3.68)

(ii) For
\psi\in\mathbb{B}_{0}
, an explicit inverse of
H_{\mu,\lambda}
restricted on imaginary axis ;
y\in[0,+\infty[
is given by

\displaystyle{H_{\mu,\lambda}^{-1}\psi(-iy)=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathcal{N}_{\mu,\lambda}(y,s)\psi(-is)ds}

(3.69)

where
\displaystyle{\mathcal{N}_{\mu,\lambda}(y,s)=\frac{1}{\lambda s}e^{-\frac{s^{2}}{2}-\frac{\mu}{\lambda}s}\int_{0}^{min(y,s)}e^{\frac{u^{2}}{2}+\frac{\mu}{\lambda}u}du}

Proof

(i) We substitue
\displaystyle{\varphi_{0}(u)=-\int_{u}^{0}\varphi_{0}^{{}^{\prime}}(s)ds}
in (3.64) to obtain

\displaystyle{\varphi_{0}^{{}^{\prime}}(y)=-\sigma e^{\frac{1}{2}(y-\rho)^{2}}\int_{-\infty}^{y}\frac{e^{-\frac{1}{2}(u-\rho)^{2}}}{u}\int_{u}^{0}\varphi_{0}^{{}^{\prime}}(s)ds;\,y\in\,]-\infty,0]}

(3.70)

By applying the Fubini theorem we get

\displaystyle{\int_{-\infty}^{y}\frac{e^{-\frac{1}{2}(u-\rho)^{2}}}{u}\int_{u}^{0}\varphi_{0}^{{}^{\prime}}(s)ds=\int_{-\infty}^{0}[\int_{-\infty}^{min(y,s)}\frac{e^{-\frac{1}{2}(u-\rho)^{2}}}{u}du]\varphi_{0}^{{}^{\prime}}(s)ds}

(3.71)

and

\displaystyle{\varphi_{0}^{{}^{\prime}}(y)=-\sigma e^{\frac{1}{2}(y-\rho)^{2}}\int_{-\infty}^{0}[\int_{-\infty}^{min(y,s)}}\displaystyle{\frac{e^{-\frac{1}{2}(u-\rho)^{2}}}{u}du]\varphi_{0}^{{}^{\prime}}(s)ds}

(3.72)

or

\displaystyle{e^{-\frac{y^{2}}{2}}\varphi_{0}^{{}^{\prime}}(y)}\displaystyle{=-\sigma e^{\frac{\rho^{2}}{2}}\int_{-\infty}^{0}[e^{\rho y}\int_{-\infty}^{min(y,s)}\frac{e^{-\frac{1}{2}(u-\rho)^{2}}}{u}du]\varphi_{0}^{{}^{\prime}}(s)ds}

(3.73)

Now, if we put :

\displaystyle{\psi(y)=\frac{\varphi_{0}^{{}^{\prime}}(y)}{y}e^{-\frac{y^{2}}{2}}\theta(y)}

(3.74)

with
\theta(y)=\left\{\begin{array}[c]{l}\,y;\quad y\in[-1,0]\\
\\
-1;\quad y\in]-\infty,-1]\\
\end{array}\right.

and if we substitute
\psi
in (3.73) then we get

\displaystyle{\psi(y)=-\sigma e^{\frac{\rho^{2}}{2}}\int_{-\infty}^{0}\mathcal{N}(y,s)\psi(s)ds}

(3.75)

Where
\mathcal{N}(y,s)
is given by (3.67).

(ii) Let
\varphi\in\mathbb{B}_{0}
and
\psi\in\mathbb{B}_{0}
, we consider the equation
H_{\mu,\lambda}\varphi=\psi(z)
i.e.

\displaystyle{i\lambda z\varphi^{"}(z)+(i\lambda z^{2}+\mu z)\varphi^{\prime}(z)=\psi(z)}

(3.76)

,

and

with

then (3.76) can be written in the following form:

\displaystyle{-\lambda yu^{\prime\prime}(y)+(\lambda y^{2}+\mu y)u^{\prime}(y)=f(y)}

(3.77)

or

\displaystyle{-u^{\prime\prime}(y)+(y+\rho)u^{\prime}(y)=\frac{f(y)}{\lambda y}}

(3.78)

with help theorem 3.4, Lemma 3.2 and a similar technique used in proof of (i) of this theorem we get (3.69).
\square

(3) The extension of integral operator associated to the restriction of
H_{\mu,\lambda}
on negative imaginary axis

to Hilbert-Scmidt operator on

\displaystyle{L_{2}(]-\infty,0],\theta(y)dy)}

Proposition 3.20
.

Le
\mathcal{N}(y,s)
=
e^{-\rho y}\frac{\theta(y)}{y}\frac{s}{\theta(s)}e^{\frac{1}{2}s^{2}}

\displaystyle{\int_{-\infty}^{min(y,s)}}

\frac{e^{-\frac{1}{2}(u-\rho)^{2}}}{u}du
be the kernel of the equation:

\displaystyle{-\lambda y\varphi^{\prime\prime}(iy)+(\lambda y^{2}+\mu y)\varphi^{\prime}(iy)=\psi(iy),y<0}

(3.79)

where
\displaystyle{\rho=\frac{\mu}{\lambda}}
then:

(i)
\mathcal{N}(y,s)
is Hilbert-Shmidt i.e.
\displaystyle{\mathcal{N}(y,s)}
belongs to

\displaystyle{L^{2}(]-\infty,0]\times]-\infty,0],\theta(y)\theta(s)dyds)}
.

(ii)
\displaystyle{\mathcal{N}(y,s)}
belongs to
\displaystyle{L^{2}(]-\infty,0]\times]-\infty,0],\theta(y)\theta(s)dyds)}
.

Proof

(i) To apply the theorem of dominated convergence, we will increase
K(y,s)
by a function
\tilde{K}(y,s)\in L^{2}(]-\infty,0]\times]-\infty,0],\theta(y)\theta(s)dyds)

For this we pose
min(y,s)=m
then we have :

\displaystyle{\mid\frac{e^{-\frac{1}{2}(u-\rho)^{2}}}{u}\mid\leq e^{-\frac{1}{2}\rho^{2}}\frac{1}{m(m-\rho)}\mid(\rho-u)e^{-\frac{1}{2}u^{2}+\rho u}\mid}

(3.80)

The function
u\longrightarrow\displaystyle{\frac{1}{u(u-\rho)}}
is bounded for
-\infty<u\leq m<0

Hence a first increase:

\mid\mathcal{N}(y,s)\mid\leq\displaystyle{e^{-\frac{1}{2}\rho^{2}}\mid\frac{e^{-\rho y}}{y}\frac{\theta(y)}{\theta(s)}se^{\frac{1}{2}s^{2}}\frac{e^{-\frac{1}{2}m^{2}+\rho^{m}}}{m(m-\rho)}\mid}

(3.81)

If
y\leq s
, we have:

m=y
et
\displaystyle{e^{\frac{1}{2}s^{2}-\frac{1}{2}y^{2}}<1}.

Then :

\mid\mathcal{N}(y,s)\mid\leq\displaystyle{e^{-\frac{1}{2}\rho^{2}}\mid\frac{\theta(y)}{y}\frac{s}{\theta(s)}\frac{1}{y(y-\rho)}}\mid

(3.82)

If
s\leq y
, we have:

m=s
et
e^{\rho(s-y)}<1.

Therefore

\mid\mathcal{N}(y,s)\mid\leq e^{-\frac{1}{2}\rho^{2}}\mid\frac{\theta(y)}{y}\frac{s}{\theta(s)}\frac{1}{s(s-\rho)}\mid

(3.83)

Finally we put :

\tilde{\mathcal{N}}(y,s)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ c }e^{-\frac{1}{2}\rho^{2}}\mid\frac{\theta(y)}{y}\frac{s}{\theta(s)}\frac{1}{y(y-\rho)}\quad pour\quad y\leq s\\
e^{-\frac{1}{2}\rho^{2}}\mid\frac{\theta(y)}{y}\frac{s}{\theta(s)}\frac{1}{s(s-\rho)}\quad pour\quad s\leq y\\
\end{array}\right.

(3.84)

to get :

\mid\mathcal{N}(y,s)\mid\leq\mid\tilde{N}(y,s)\mid

(3.85)

it remains to show that
\tilde{N}(y,s)\in L^{2}(]-\infty,0]\times]-\infty,0],\theta(y)\theta(s)dyds)

Let
\Delta=]-\infty,0]\times]-\infty,0]-]-1,0]\times]-1,0],\Delta_{1}=\{(y,s)\in\Delta;y\leq s\}
et

\Delta_{2}=\{(y,s)\in\Delta;s\leq y\}
. Then

\displaystyle{\int_{-\infty}^{0}\theta(y)dy}\displaystyle{\int_{-\infty}^{0}\theta(s)\mid\tilde{N}(y,s)\mid^{2}ds}=

\displaystyle{\int_{\Delta_{1}}\theta(y)\theta(s)\mid\frac{\theta(y)}{y}\frac{s}{\theta(s)}\frac{1}{y(y-\rho)}\mid^{2}dyds}+\displaystyle{\int_{\Delta_{2}}\theta(y)\theta(s)\mid\frac{\theta(y)}{y}\frac{s}{\theta(s)}\frac{1}{s(s-\rho)}\mid^{2}dyds}

As

and the function

is integrable at the origin, we deduce that:

\displaystyle{\int_{-\infty}^{0}\theta(y)dy}\displaystyle{\int_{-\infty}^{0}\theta(s)\mid\tilde{N}(y,s)\mid^{2}ds<+\infty}

(3.86)

then
\mathcal{N}(y,s)
is a kernel of Hilbert-Shmidt.

We note that the functions :

y\rightarrow\displaystyle{\int_{-\infty}^{y}\frac{e^{-\frac{1}{2}(u-\rho)^{2}}}{u}du};\quad y\leq s

and

are decreasing and tend to zero respectively when

and

,we deduce that:

-\mathcal{N}(y,s)
non negative.
\square

Remark 3.21
.

It follows that we can give an explicit form of inverse of

\displaystyle{N_{0,\mu,\lambda}(y,s)=\frac{1}{\lambda s}e^{\frac{-s^{2}}{2}-\frac{\mu}{\lambda}s}\int_{0}^{min(y,s)}e^{\frac{u^{2}}{2}+\frac{\mu}{\lambda}u}du}

(3.88)

Let
\displaystyle{L_{2}([0,+\infty[,e^{-x^{2}-2\omega x}dx)}
be the space of square integrable functions with respect to the measure

.

Proposition 3.23
.

(continuity of
H_{\mu,\lambda}^{-1}
with respect to
\lambda
)

Let
\mu>0
and
\displaystyle{f:\mathbb{R}\longrightarrow\mathcal{B}_{0};\lambda\mapsto f(\lambda)=H_{\mu,\lambda}^{-1}}
, then
f
is continuous.

Proof

As for any
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
and
\mu>0
we have
\displaystyle{\mu\mid\mid\varphi\mid\mid\leq\mid\mid H_{\mu,\lambda}\varphi\mid\mid}
on
\mathcal{B}_{0}
then

\displaystyle{\mid\mid H_{\mu,\lambda}^{-1}\psi\mid\mid\leq\frac{1}{\mu}\mid\mid\psi\mid\mid}

\forall\,\psi\in\mathcal{B}_{0}
. (*)

Now, for
\lambda_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
we have :

\displaystyle{H_{\mu,\lambda}^{-1}-H_{\mu,\lambda_{0}}^{-1}=i(\lambda_{0}-\lambda)H_{\mu,\lambda}^{-1}A^{*}(A+A^{*})H_{\mu,\lambda_{0}}^{-1}}
.

By applying (*) we get

\displaystyle{\mid\mid H_{\mu,\lambda}^{-1}-H_{\mu,\lambda_{0}}^{-1}\mid\mid\leq\frac{1}{\mu}\mid\mid A^{*}(A+A^{*})H_{\mu,\lambda_{0}}^{-1}\mid\mid.\mid\lambda_{0}-\lambda\mid}
.

and as

\displaystyle{\mid\mid A^{*}(A+A^{*})H_{\mu,\lambda_{0}}^{-1}\mid\mid=\frac{1}{\mid\lambda_{0}\mid}(1+\mu\mid\mid A^{*}AH_{\mu,\lambda_{0}}^{-1}\mid\mid}
.

From the density of the polynomial set
\mathcal{P}_{0}
in
\mathbb{B}_{0}
, the equality of minimal domain with maximal domain of
\displaystyle{H_{\mu,\lambda}}
and
\displaystyle{[A^{*}A]^{-1}(\mathcal{P}_{0})=\mathcal{P}_{0}}
, we deduce the following fundamental inequality :

\displaystyle{\forall\,\epsilon>0,(\mu-\epsilon)\mid\mid A^{*}A\varphi\mid\mid\leq\mid\mid H_{\mu,\lambda}\varphi\mid\mid+\frac{\mid\lambda\mid^{2}}{4\epsilon}\mid\mid\varphi\mid\mid\,\forall\,\varphi\in D(H_{\mu,\lambda})}
(**)

We choose
\displaystyle{\epsilon=\frac{\mu}{2}}
to get :

\displaystyle{\frac{1}{\mid\lambda_{0}\mid}(1+\mu\mid\mid A^{*}AH_{\mu,\lambda_{0}}^{-1}\mid\mid)\leq\frac{1}{\mid\lambda_{0}\mid}(3+\frac{\mid\lambda_{0}\mid^{2}}{\mu})}
.

Consequently, we get:

\displaystyle{\mid\mid H_{\mu,\lambda}^{-1}-H_{\mu,\lambda_{0}}^{-1}\mid\mid\leq\frac{1}{\mu}\frac{1}{\mid\lambda_{0}\mid}(3+\frac{\mid\lambda_{0}\mid^{2}}{\mu})\mid\lambda_{0}-\lambda\mid}

i.e the continuity of
H_{\mu,\lambda}^{-1}
in
\lambda_{0}\neq 0
.

Now for
\lambda_{0}=0
, we have:

\displaystyle{\mid\mid H_{\mu,\lambda}^{-1}\psi-\frac{1}{\mu}[A^{*}A]^{-1}\psi\mid\mid\leq\mid\mid H_{\mu,\lambda_{0}}^{-1}\mid\mid.\mid\mid\psi-\frac{1}{\mu}H_{\mu,\lambda}[A^{*}A]^{-1}\psi\mid\mid\forall\,[A^{*}A]^{-1}\psi\in D(H_{\mu,\lambda})}

i.e.
\forall\,\psi\in A^{*}A[D(H_{\mu,\lambda})]
.

Hence we use the fundamental inequality (**) to get:

\displaystyle{\mid\mid H_{\mu,\lambda}^{-1}\psi-\frac{1}{\mu}[A^{*}A]^{-1}\psi\mid\mid\leq\frac{\mid\lambda\mid}{\mu^{2}}\mid\mid A^{*}(A+A^{*})A[A^{*}A]^{-1}\psi\mid\mid}
which converges to zero as
\lambda\longrightarrow 0

\forall\,\psi\in A^{*}A[D(H_{\mu,\lambda})]
.

Now, as
\displaystyle{A^{*}A[D(H_{\mu,\lambda})]}
is dense in
\mathbb{B}_{0}
, we deduce the continuity of
f
at zero.
\square

3.5
what class Carleman operators belong the inverse of
H_{\mu,\lambda}
?

\bigstar

On the factorization of
H_{\mu,\lambda}^{-1}

Which bounded linear operator on a Hilbert space can be factored as the product of finitely many normal operators ? What is the answer if is normal operators is replaced by involutions ? partial isometrics ? or other classes of familiar operators ? Just as in the case of the factorization of integers, polynomials, or other objects in mathematics, such operator factorization problems seem to arise very naturally in the course of study of operators.

In the cases when they are solved, the solutions are usually neat and elegant; otherwise, they pose interesting and challenging questions whose solutions may lead us to a deeper understanding of the nature of the operators under consideration.

For the products of two Hermitian operators on finite-dimensional spaces, we have the following classical characterization

Theorem 3.24
.

The following statements are equivalent for a finite matrix
T
:

(1)
T
is the product of two Hermitian matrices;

(2)
T
is the product of two Hermitian matrices, one of which is nonsingular;

(3)
T
is similar to a matrix with real entries;

(4)
T
is similar to
T^{*}
.

Proof

The proof is an easy exercise in linear algebra. In the context of infinite- dimensional spaces, the implication
(1)\Longrightarrow(4)
is no longer true. As a consolation, it was conjectured that
T
is similar to
T^{*}
if
T
is the product of two Hermitian Fredholm operators. This seems still to be open.

As for the implication
(4)\Longrightarrow(1)
despite the abundance of supporting special cases, its validity is still unconfirmed even under the extra assumption that
T
is invertible.
\square

Remark 3.25
.

The following conditions are equivalent for an operator
T
on an infinite-dimensional space:

(1)
T
is a commutator;

(2)
T
is not the sum of nonzero scalar and a compact operator.

In this context, the products of commutators seem not to have been considered. It would also be interesting to characterize products of a special kind of commutators: self-commutators, operators of the form
T^{*}T-TT^{*}
.

Remark 3.26
.

Any compact operator on an infinite-dimensional space is the product of finitely many nonnegative operators.

For our operator
H_{\mu,\lambda}
, we will answered to following question: Its inverse
H_{\mu,\lambda}^{-1}
can be factorized as the product of two operators of Carleman’s classes ?

Let
(\mu,\lambda)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
H_{\mu,\lambda}=\mu H_{0}+i\lambda H_{1}
with
H_{0}=A^{*}A
and
H_{1}=A^{*}(A+A^{*})A
then we can to write
H_{0}^{-1}H_{1}
but not
H_{1}H_{0}^{-1}
because the domain of
H_{0}
and the domain of
H_{1}
are not included in each other, nor is the domain of their commutator.

Observing that
H_{0}^{-1}(\mathcal{P})=\mathcal{P}=H_{0}(\mathcal{P})
then
H_{1}H_{0}^{-1}
is well defined on the polynomial space
\mathcal{P}
and we have:

\displaystyle{\forall\,p\in\mathcal{P},H_{\mu,\lambda}H_{0}^{-1}p=\mu p+i\lambda H_{1}H_{0}^{-1}p=\mu p+i\lambda A^{*}A^{2}H_{0}^{-1}p+i\lambda A^{*^{2}}AH_{0}^{-1}p}

\displaystyle{=\mu p+i\lambda(AA^{*}-I)AH_{0}^{-1}p+i\lambda A^{*}p=\mu p+i\lambda(A+A^{*})p-i\lambda AH_{0}^{-1}p}
.

In particular, we get:

\displaystyle{\mid\Re e<H_{\mu,\lambda}H_{0}^{-1}p,p>\mid\geq\mu\mid\mid p\mid\mid^{2}-\mid\lambda\mid\mid\Im m<AH_{0}^{-1}p,p>\mid\forall\,p\in\mathcal{P}}
(
\bigstar_{1}
)

and

\displaystyle{\mid\mid H_{\mu,\lambda}H_{0}^{-1}p\mid\mid.\mid\mid p\mid\mid\geq\mu\mid\mid p\mid\mid^{2}-\mid\lambda\mid\mid\mid AH_{0}^{-1}p\mid\mid.\mid\mid p\mid\mid\forall\,p\in\mathcal{P}}
(
\bigstar_{2}
)

Let
\displaystyle{p(z)=\sum_{k=1}^{n}a_{k}\frac{z^{k}}{\sqrt{k!}}}
then from (
\bigstar_{1}
), (
\bigstar_{2}
) and :

(i)
\displaystyle{AH_{0}^{-1}p(z)=\sum_{k=1}^{n}a_{k}AH_{0}^{-1}\frac{z^{k}}{\sqrt{k!}}=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\frac{a_{k}}{\sqrt{k}}\frac{z^{k-1}}{\sqrt{(k-1)!}};}

(ii)
\displaystyle{\mid\mid A\varphi\mid\mid^{2}=<A^{*}A\varphi,\varphi>=<H_{0}\varphi,\varphi>;\varphi\in D(H_{0})}
,

we deduce that

\displaystyle{\mid\mid AH_{0}^{-1}p\mid\mid^{2}\leq\mid\mid p\mid\mid^{2}\forall\,p\in\mathcal{P};}
(
\bigstar_{3}
)

\displaystyle{\mid\mid A\varphi\mid\mid\leq\mid\mid H_{0}\varphi\mid\mid^{\frac{1}{2}}\mid\mid\varphi\mid\mid^{\frac{1}{2}},\forall\,\varphi\in D(H_{0});}
(
\bigstar_{4}
)

\displaystyle{\mid\mid AH_{0}^{-1}p\mid\mid\leq\mid\mid\varphi\mid\mid^{\frac{1}{2}}\mid\mid H_{0}^{-1}p\mid\mid^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq\frac{\epsilon}{\mid\lambda\mid}\mid\mid p\mid\mid+\frac{\mid\lambda\mid}{\mid 4\epsilon\mid}\mid\mid H_{0}^{-1}p\mid\mid.}
(
\bigstar_{5}
)

\displaystyle{(\mu-\epsilon)\mid\mid p\mid\mid\leq\mid\mid H_{\mu,\lambda}H_{0}^{-1}p\mid\mid+\frac{\mid\lambda\mid^{2}}{4\epsilon}\mid\mid H_{0}^{-1}p\mid\mid\forall\,p\in\mathcal{P}}
(
\bigstar_{6}
)

Now, as
\mathcal{P}
is dense in
\mathcal{B}_{0}
and the maximal domain of
H_{\mu,\lambda}
coincides with its minimal domain, we deduce the following fundamental inequality:

\displaystyle{\forall\epsilon>0,(\mu-\epsilon)\mid\mid H_{0}H_{\mu,\lambda}^{-1}\psi\mid\mid\,\,\leq\,\,(1+\frac{\mid\lambda\mid^{2}}{4\mid\mu\epsilon\mid})\mid\mid\psi\mid\mid\forall\,\psi\in\mathcal{B}_{0}}
(
\bigstar_{7}
)

We summarize the results of above inequalities as follows :

Theorem 3.27
.

(i)
A
is
\frac{1}{2}
-subordinated to
H_{0}
,

(ii)
D(H_{\mu,\lambda})\subset D(H_{0})
,

(iii)
H_{0}
is subordinated to
H_{\mu,\lambda}
,

(iv) the operator
T=H_{0}H_{\mu,\lambda}^{-1}
is bounded on Bargmann space
\mathcal{B}_{0}
and
H_{\mu,\lambda}^{-1}
is factored as product of
H_{0}^{-1}
with
T
,

(v) the operators
H_{0}^{-1}
and
H_{\mu,\lambda}^{-1}
are of Carleman class of order
1+\epsilon
,
\forall\,\epsilon>0
.

Open question

Is there exist a strictly positive bounded operator
\mathbb{T}
acting on Bargmann space such that
\mathbb{T}H_{\mu,\lambda}^{-1}=H_{\mu,\lambda}^{-1^{*}}\mathbb{T}
to apply the followiong Krein’s theorem:

Theorem 3.28
.

(see
[Krein]
)

Let
\mathcal{H}
be an infinite dimensional, separable, complex Hilbert space and let
\mathcal{C}_{p}=\mathcal{C}_{p}(\mathcal{H})
be the Schatten-von Neumann class of compact operators acting on
\mathcal{H}
and let
\mathbb{M}\in\mathcal{C}_{p}(\mathcal{H})
be a linear bounded operator with the property that there exists a strictly positive bounded operator
\mathbb{A}
such that
\mathbb{A}\mathbb{M}=\mathbb{M}^{*}\mathbb{A}
. Then the spectrum of
\mathbb{M}
is real and for every non-zero eigenvalue
\lambda
, if
(\mathbb{M}-\lambda I)^{m}f=0
for some
m>1
, then
(\mathbb{M}-\lambda I)f=0
.

Moreover, the eigenvectors of
\mathbb{M}^{*}
, including the null vectors, span
\mathcal{H}
.

Although complex symmetric matrices (complex matrices coinciding with their transposes) are as ubiquitous as Hermitian matrices (those coinciding with their
complex adjoint), the first class is less well-known than the second. The matrix associated to
H_{\mu,\lambda}
belongs to first class.Takagi , was the first who had remarked that the antilinear eigenvalue problem
T\varphi=\sigma\bar{\varphi}
, where
T
is a complex symmetric matrix and
\bar{\varphi}
denotes complex conjugation, entry by entry, of the vector
\varphi
, solves a fundamental interpolation problem for bounded analytic functions in the disk. He had remarked there that the largest such positive skew-eigenvalue
\sigma
coincides with the operator norm of
T
. Later on, this observation was extended to bounded linear operators with certain symmetries. About half a century ago, Glazman laid the foundations of the theory of unbounded complex symmetric operators. We will tested the Glazman’s theory on our operator
H_{\mu,\lambda}
.

We begin by recalling a few definitions and facts about complex symmetric operators.

Definition 3.29
.

We consider a separable Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
which carries a conjugation operator
\displaystyle{\mathcal{C}:\mathcal{H}\longrightarrow\mathcal{H}}
(an antilinear operator satisfying the conditions
\displaystyle{\mathcal{C}^{2}=I}
and
\displaystyle{<\mathcal{C}\varphi,\mathcal{C}\psi>=<\varphi,\psi>\,\forall\,(\varphi,\psi)\in\mathcal{H}\times\mathcal{H}}
.

For a fixed
\mathcal{C}
, we define the transpose
{}^{t}T
of a bounded linear operator
T
to be
{}^{t}T=\mathcal{C}T^{*}\mathcal{C}
where
T^{*}
is adjoint of
T
.

We say that
T
is
\mathcal{C}
-symmetric if
T=^{t}T
(equivalently, if
\displaystyle{\mathcal{C}T=T\mathcal{C}}
). More generally, we say that T is complex symmetric if there exists a
\mathcal{C}
such that
T
is
\mathcal{C}
-symmetric.

We say that a densely defined, closed graph operator is
\mathcal{C}
-symmetric if
\displaystyle{T\subset\mathcal{C}T^{*}\mathcal{C}}
and
\mathcal{C}
-selfadjoint if
\displaystyle{T=\mathcal{C}T^{*}\mathcal{C}}
.

By applying the Glazman’s theory on our operator
H_{\mu,\lambda}
then we obtain the following theorem:

Theorem 3.30
.

Let
\mu>0
and
\displaystyle{H_{\mu,\lambda}:D(H_{\mu,\lambda})\longrightarrow\mathcal{B}_{0}}
then

(i)
H_{\mu,\lambda}
is an unbounded
\mathcal{C}
-selfadjoint operator.

(ii) There exists an orthonormal basis
(\varphi_{n})_{n=0}
of
\mathcal{B}_{0}
consisting of solutions of the antilinear eigenvalue problem
\displaystyle{H_{\mu,\lambda}\varphi_{n}=\sigma{{}_{n}}\mathcal{C}\varphi_{n}}
where
(\sigma{{}_{n}})_{n=0}
is an increasing sequence of positive numbers tending to
\infty
.

(iii)
\displaystyle{\mid\mid H_{\mu,\lambda}^{-1}\mid\mid=\frac{1}{inf_{n}\sigma_{n}}}

Proof

(i) As
H_{\mu,\lambda}=H_{\mu,-\lambda}^{*}
then
H_{\mu,\lambda}
is
\mathcal{C}
-symmetric and as
H_{\mu,\lambda}^{-1}
exists then by applying a criterion which goes back to Zhikhar : ¡¡ if a
\mathcal{C}
-symmetric operator
T
with dense domain
D(T)

\displaystyle{T:D(T)\subset\mathcal{H}\longrightarrow\mathcal{H}}
satisfies
\displaystyle{(T-zI)D(T)=\mathcal{H}}
for some complex number
z
, then
T
is
\mathcal{C}
-selfadjoint ¿¿, we deduce that
H_{\mu,\lambda}
is an unbounded
\mathcal{C}
-selfadjoint operator.

(i) and (ii) is a direct consequence of results established by Garcia and Putinar in
[Garcia et al]
.
\square

3.6
Explicit inversion of
H_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}
on
[0,-i\frac{\lambda}{\lambda^{\prime}}]
and convergence of spectral radius of
H_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}^{-1}
to spectral radius of
H_{\mu,\lambda}^{-1}
as
\lambda^{\prime}\longrightarrow 0

Using the same above technique used to get the kernel of
H_{\mu,\lambda}^{-1}
on
]-\infty,0]
, we explicit the inverse of
H_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}
on
[0,-i\frac{\lambda}{\lambda^{\prime}}]
.

Let
\psi\in\mathbb{B}_{0}
and
\phi\in D(H_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda})
, we consider the equation
H_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}\phi=\psi
which can be written under following form :

\displaystyle{(\lambda^{\prime}z^{2}+i\lambda z)\phi^{\prime\prime}(z)+(i\lambda z^{2}+\mu z)\phi^{\prime}(z)=\psi(z)}

(3.89)

Choosing the straight line connecting
-i\rho^{\prime},z\in\mathbb{C}
parametrized by

\displaystyle{\gamma:[0,1]\rightarrow\mathbb{C},\gamma(t)=-i\rho^{\prime}+t(z+i\rho^{\prime})\quad\quad(\gamma(0)=-i\rho^{\prime},\gamma(1)=z)}

(3.90)

If we define
\displaystyle{\int_{-i\rho^{\prime}}^{z}\psi(\xi)d\xi:=\int_{\gamma}\psi(\xi)d\xi:=\int_{0}^{1}}\psi(\gamma(t))\gamma^{\prime}(t)dt
then our equation can be transformed to following integral equation :

\displaystyle{\phi(z)=\frac{1}{\lambda^{\prime}}\int_{-i\rho^{\prime}}^{z}e^{-i\rho^{\prime}\eta}(\eta+i\rho^{\prime})^{-(\delta+1)}[\int_{-i\rho^{\prime}}^{\eta}e^{i\rho^{\prime}\xi}(\xi+i\rho^{\prime})^{\delta}\frac{\psi(\xi)}{\xi}d\xi]d\eta}

(3.91)

However, the integral representation of
\phi(z)
in this last equation is hard to study in
\mathbb{C}
for some existing results on eigenvalues and eigenfunctions of our operators. To overcome this difficulty, the study of integral operator is restricted on negative imaginary axis by setting :

z=-iy
,
u(y)=\phi(-iy)
and
f(y)=\psi(-iy)
with
y\in[0,\rho^{\prime}]
then (3.89) can be written in the following form:

\displaystyle{(\lambda^{\prime}y^{2}-\lambda y)u^{\prime\prime}(y)+(\lambda y^{2}+\mu y)u^{\prime}(y)=f(y)}

(3.92)

As
\psi\in\mathbb{B}_{0}
then
f(0)=0
and if we put
\delta=\rho^{\prime}(\rho+\rho^{\prime})-1
,
\eta=-is
and
\xi=y_{1}
with
s\in[0,y]
and
y_{1}\in[s,\rho^{\prime}]
then (3.91) can be transformed to following integral equation

\displaystyle{\phi(-iy)=\frac{1}{\lambda^{\prime}}\int_{0}^{y}e^{-\rho^{\prime}s}(\rho^{\prime}-s)^{-(\delta+1)}\int_{s}^{\rho^{\prime}}e^{\rho^{\prime}y_{1}}(\rho^{\prime}-y_{1})^{\delta}\frac{\psi(-iy_{1})}{y_{1}}dy_{1}ds}

(3.93)

then we get

\displaystyle{H_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}^{-1}\psi(-iy)=\int_{0}^{\rho^{\prime}}N_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}(y,y_{1})\psi(-iy_{1})dy_{1}}

(3.94)

with

\displaystyle{N_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}(y,y_{1})=\frac{1}{\lambda^{\prime}y_{1}}e^{\rho^{\prime}y_{1}}(\rho^{\prime}-y_{1})^{\delta}\int_{0}^{min(y,y_{1})}e^{-\rho^{\prime}s}(\rho^{\prime}-s)^{-(\delta+1)}ds}

(3.95)

or

\displaystyle{N_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}(y,y_{1})=\frac{1}{\lambda y_{1}}e^{\rho^{\prime}y_{1}}(1-\frac{y_{1}}{\rho^{\prime}})^{\delta}\int_{0}^{min(y,y_{1})}e^{-\rho^{\prime}s}(1-\frac{s}{\rho^{\prime}})^{-(\delta+1)}ds}

(3.96)

Let
\Upsilon=min(y,y_{1})
and
\displaystyle{\Theta(\Upsilon)=\int_{0}^{\Upsilon}e^{-\rho^{\prime}s}(1-\frac{s}{\rho^{\prime}})^{-(\delta+1)}ds}

then we get the following elementary properties on the function
\Theta(\Upsilon)
near to points zero and
\rho^{\prime}
respectively:

(i)
\displaystyle{\Theta(\Upsilon)\equiv\Upsilon}
near zero.

(ii)
\displaystyle{\Theta(\Upsilon)\equiv(\rho^{\prime}-\Upsilon)^{-\delta}}
near
\rho^{\prime}
.

Theorem 3.31
.

Let
L_{2}([0,\rho^{\prime}],r(y)dy)
be the space of square integrable functions with respect to the weight
r(y)=e^{2\rho^{\prime}y}(1-\frac{y}{\rho^{\prime}})^{2\delta};\rho^{\prime}=\frac{\lambda}{\lambda^{\prime}}
and
\delta=\rho^{\prime}(\rho+\rho^{\prime})-1
.

Then for
\lambda^{\prime}>0
,
\mu>0
and
\delta\geq 0

H_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}^{-1}
can be extended to Hilbert-Schmidt operator of
L_{2}([0,\rho^{\prime}],r(y)dy)
to
L_{2}([0,\rho^{\prime}],r(y)dy)
.

Proof (see follow)

The integral operator

H_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}^{-1}

on

\displaystyle{[0,-i\frac{\lambda}{\lambda^{\prime}}]}

is extended to Hilbert-Scmidt operator on

\displaystyle{L_{2}([0,-i\frac{\lambda}{\lambda^{\prime}}],r(y)dy)}

We wiil give an explicit inversion of
H_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}
on
[0,-i\frac{\lambda}{\lambda^{\prime}}]
.

Setting
\rho^{\prime}=\frac{\lambda}{\lambda^{\prime}}
,
\rho=\frac{\mu}{\lambda}
and
\delta=\rho^{\prime}(\rho+\rho^{\prime})-1
for
\lambda^{\prime}\neq 0
and
\lambda\neq 0
.

We begin by to explicit the inverse of
H_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}
on
[0,-i\frac{\lambda}{\lambda^{\prime}}]
.

Let
\psi\in\mathbb{B}_{0}
and
\phi\in D(H_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}
, we consider the equation
H_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}\phi=\psi
which can be written under following form :

\displaystyle{(\lambda^{\prime}z^{2}+i\lambda z)\phi^{\prime\prime}(z)=(i\lambda z^{2}+\mu z)\phi^{\prime}(z)=\psi(z)}

(B.1)

Let
\psi\in\mathbb{B}_{0}
and choosing the straight line connecting
-i\rho^{\prime},z\in\mathbb{C}
parametrized by

\displaystyle{\gamma:[0,1]\rightarrow\mathbb{C},\gamma(t)=-i\rho^{\prime}+t(z+i\rho^{\prime})\quad\quad(\gamma(0)=-i\rho^{\prime},\gamma(1)=z)}

If we define
\displaystyle{\int_{-i\rho^{\prime}}^{z}\psi(\xi)d\xi:=\int_{\gamma}\psi(\xi)d\xi:=\int_{0}^{1}}\psi(\gamma(t))\gamma^{\prime}(t)dt
then the equation (B.1) can be transformed to following integral equation :

\displaystyle{\phi(z)=\frac{1}{\lambda^{\prime}}\int_{-i\rho^{\prime}}^{z}e^{-i\rho^{\prime}\eta}(\eta+i\rho^{\prime})^{-(\delta+1)}[\int_{-i\rho^{\prime}}^{\eta}e^{i\rho^{\prime}\xi}(\xi+i\rho^{\prime})^{\delta}\frac{\psi(\xi)}{\xi}d\xi]d\eta}

(B.2)

However, the integral representation of
\phi(z)
in this last equation (B.2) is hard to study in
\mathbb{C}
for some existing results on eigenvalues and eigenfunctions of our operators. To overcome this difficulty, the study of (B.2) is restricted on negative imaginary axis by setting :

z=-iy
,
u(y)=\phi(-iy)
and
f(y)=\psi(-iy)
with
y\in[0,\rho^{\prime}]
then (B.1) can be written in the following form:

\displaystyle{(\lambda^{\prime}y^{2}-\lambda y)u^{\prime\prime}(y)+(\lambda y^{2}+\mu y)u^{\prime}(y)=f(y)}

(B.3)

As
\psi\in\mathbb{B}_{0}
then
f(0)=0
and if we put
\eta=-is
and
\xi=y_{1}
with ,
s\in[0,y]
and
y_{1}\in[s,\rho^{\prime}]
then (B.3) can be transformed to following integral equation

\displaystyle{\phi(-iy)=\frac{1}{\lambda^{\prime}}\int_{0}^{y}e^{-\rho^{\prime}s}(\rho^{\prime}-s)^{-(\delta+1)}\int_{s}^{\rho^{\prime}}e^{\rho^{\prime}y_{1}}(\rho^{\prime}-y_{1})^{\delta}\frac{\psi(-iy_{1})}{y_{1}}dy_{1}ds}

(B.4)

then we get

\displaystyle{K_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}\psi(-iy)=\int_{0}^{\rho^{\prime}}N_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}(y,y_{1})\psi(-iy_{1})dy_{1}}

(B.5)

with

\displaystyle{N_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}(y,y_{1})=\frac{1}{\lambda^{\prime}y_{1}}e^{\rho^{\prime}y_{1}}(\rho^{\prime}-y_{1})^{\delta}\int_{0}^{min(y,y_{1})}e^{-\rho^{\prime}s}(\rho^{\prime}-s)^{-(\delta+1)}ds}

(B.6)

or

\displaystyle{N_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}(y,y_{1})=\frac{1}{\lambda y_{1}}e^{\rho^{\prime}y_{1}}(1-\frac{y_{1}}{\rho^{\prime}})^{\delta}\int_{0}^{min(y,y_{1})}e^{-\rho^{\prime}s}(1-\frac{s}{\rho^{\prime}})^{-(\delta+1)}ds}

(B.7)

Let
\Upsilon=min(y,y_{1})
and
\displaystyle{\Theta(\Upsilon)=\int_{0}^{\Upsilon}e^{-\rho^{\prime}s}(1-\frac{s}{\rho^{\prime}})^{-(\delta+1)}ds}

then we get the following elementary properties on the function
\Theta(\Upsilon)
near to points zero and
\rho^{\prime}
respectively:

Let
\Upsilon=min(y,y_{1})
et
\displaystyle{\Theta(\Upsilon)=\int_{0}^{\Upsilon}e^{-\rho^{\prime}s}(1-\frac{s}{\rho^{\prime}})^{-(\delta+1)}ds}
then

(i)
\displaystyle{\Theta(\Upsilon)\equiv\Upsilon}
near zero.

(ii)
\displaystyle{\Theta(\Upsilon)\sim(\rho^{\prime}-\Upsilon)^{-\delta}}
near
\rho^{\prime}
.

To give an extension of
K_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}
to
L_{2}([0,\rho^{\prime}],r(y)dy)
, we consider

Let
L_{2}([0,\rho^{\prime}],r(y)dy)
be the space of square integrable functions with respect to the weight
r(y)=e^{2\rho^{\prime}y}(1-\frac{y}{\rho^{\prime}})^{2\delta};\rho^{\prime}=\frac{\lambda}{\lambda^{\prime}}
and
\delta=\rho^{\prime}(\rho+\rho^{\prime})-1
.

By using the above lemma, we deduce the following theorem:

Theorem 3.32
.

For
\lambda^{\prime}>0
,
\mu>0
and
\delta\geq 0

K_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}
can be extended to Hilbert-Schmidt operator of
L_{2}([0,\rho^{\prime}],r(y)dy)
to
L_{2}([0,\rho^{\prime}],r(y)dy)
.

Proof

Let
\displaystyle{r(y)=e^{2\rho^{\prime}y}(1-\frac{y}{\rho^{\prime}})^{2\delta};\delta=\rho^{\prime}(\rho+\rho^{\prime})-1}
then

\displaystyle{K_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}\psi(-iy)=\int_{0}^{\rho^{\prime}}N_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}(y,y_{1})\psi(-iy_{1})dy_{1}}

with

\displaystyle{N_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}(y,y_{1})=\frac{1}{\lambda y_{1}}e^{\rho^{\prime}y_{1}}(1-\frac{y_{1}}{\rho^{\prime}})^{\delta}\int_{0}^{min(y,y_{1})}e^{-\rho^{\prime}s}(1-\frac{s}{\rho^{\prime}})^{-(\delta+1)}ds}

can be written under the following form :

\displaystyle{K_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}\psi(-iy)=\int_{0}^{\rho^{\prime}}\tilde{N}_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}(y,y_{1})\psi(-iy_{1})r(y_{1})dy_{1}}

(B.8)

with

\displaystyle{\tilde{N}_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}(y,y_{1})=\frac{1}{\lambda y_{1}\sqrt{r(y_{1})}}\int_{0}^{min(y,y_{1})}e^{-\rho^{\prime}s}(1-\frac{s}{\rho^{\prime}})^{-(\delta+1)}ds}

(B.9)

or

\displaystyle{\tilde{N}_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}(y,y_{1})=\frac{1}{\lambda y_{1}\sqrt{r(y_{1})}}\Theta(min(y,y_{1}))}

(B.10)

Now we consider the following integral:

\displaystyle{\mathbb{I}=\int_{0}^{\rho^{\prime}}\int_{0}^{\rho^{\prime}}\tilde{N}_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}^{2}(y,y_{1})r(y)r(y_{1})dydy_{1}}

(B.11)

or

\displaystyle{\mathbb{I}=\int_{0}^{\rho^{\prime}}\int_{0}^{\rho^{\prime}}\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}y_{1}^{2}}\Theta^{2}(min(y,y_{1}))r(y)dydy_{1}}

(B.12)

By applying Fubini theoren then (B.10) can be written as :

\displaystyle{\mathbb{I}=\int_{0}^{\rho^{\prime}}\int_{0}^{y_{1}}\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}y_{1}^{2}}\Theta^{2}(min(y,y_{1}))r(y)dydy_{1}+\int_{0}^{\rho^{\prime}}\int_{y_{1}}^{\rho^{\prime}}\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}y_{1}^{2}}\Theta^{2}(min(y,y_{1}))r(y)dydy_{1}}

and we have:

\displaystyle{\mathbb{I}=\int_{0}^{\rho^{\prime}}\int_{0}^{y_{1}}\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}y_{1}^{2}}\Theta^{2}(y)r(y)dydy_{1}+\int_{0}^{\rho^{\prime}}\int_{y_{1}}^{\rho^{\prime}}\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}y_{1}^{2}}\Theta^{2}(y_{1})r(y)dydy_{1}=\mathbb{I}_{1}+\mathbb{I}_{2}}

with

\displaystyle{\mathbb{I}_{1}=\int_{0}^{\rho^{\prime}}\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}y_{1}^{2}}\int_{0}^{y_{1}}\Theta^{2}(y)r(y)dydy_{1}}

(B.13)

and

\displaystyle{\mathbb{I}_{2}=\int_{0}^{\rho^{\prime}}\frac{\Theta^{2}(y_{1})}{\lambda^{2}y_{1}^{2}}\int_{y_{1}}^{\rho^{\prime}}r(y)dydy_{1}}

(B.14)

Now we remark that

(i) For
y_{1}
near zero we have
\displaystyle{\int_{0}^{y_{1}}\Theta^{2}(y)r(y)dy\equiv y_{1}^{3}}

and

(ii) For
y_{1}
near
\rho^{\prime}
, the function
y_{1}\rightarrow\displaystyle{\int_{0}^{y_{1}}\Theta^{2}(y)r(y)dy}
is continuous.

then from (i) and (ii), we deduce that the first integral (B.11) converges. In similar way, we verify that the second integral (B.12) converges and consequently the operator
K_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}
is Hilbert-Schmidt on
L_{2}([0,\rho^{\prime}],r(y)dy)
.
\square

We summary some above results in the following theorem :

Theorem 3.33
.

i) Let
\displaystyle{N_{\rho^{\prime}}(y,y_{1})=\frac{1}{\lambda y_{1}\sqrt{r(y_{1})}}\int_{0}^{min(y,y_{1})}e^{-\rho^{\prime}s}(1-\frac{s}{\rho^{\prime}})^{-(\delta+1)}ds}
then

the function
\rho^{\prime}\rightarrow N_{\rho^{\prime}}
is decreasing.

ii) Let
\displaystyle{N_{\rho^{\prime}}(y,y_{1})=\frac{1}{\lambda y_{1}}e^{\frac{-y_{1}^{2}}{2}-\frac{\mu}{\lambda}y_{1}}\int_{0}^{min(y,y_{1})}e^{\frac{s^{2}}{2}+\frac{\mu}{\lambda}s}ds}
then

\displaystyle{Lim\quad\int_{0}^{\rho^{\prime}}N_{\rho^{\prime}}(y,y_{1})\psi(-iy_{1})dy_{1}=\int_{0}^{+\infty}N_{\mu,\lambda}(y,y_{1})\psi(-iy_{1})dy_{1}}
as
\rho^{\prime}\longrightarrow+\infty

iii) The integral operator
K_{\rho^{\prime}}
can be extended to Hilbert-Schmidt operator of

\displaystyle{L_{2}([0,+\infty[,r_{\infty}(y)dy)}
to
\displaystyle{L_{2}([0,+\infty[,r_{\infty}(y)dy)}
.

iv) On
\displaystyle{L_{2}([0,+\infty[,r_{\infty}(y)dy)}
, the integral operator
K_{\rho^{\prime}}
converges to integral operator
K_{0,\mu,\lambda}
as
\rho^{\prime}
goes to infinity with respect to Hilbert-Schmidt norm.

v) Let
\Omega(\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda)
be the spectral radius of
K_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}
of kernel
N_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}
and let
\Omega(0,\mu,\lambda)
be the spectral radius of
K_{0,\mu,\lambda}
of kernel
N_{0,\mu,\lambda}
then

Lim\quad\Omega(\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda)=\Omega(0,\mu,\lambda)
as
\lambda^{\prime}\rightarrow 0
or
\rho^{\prime}\rightarrow+\infty
.

vi) The functions
\mu\rightarrow\tilde{N}_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}
and
\mu\rightarrow\Omega(\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda)
are creasing with respect to
\mu
.

vii) The function
\mu\rightarrow\Omega(\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda)
is analytic on the whole real line with respect to
\mu
and the function
\mu\rightarrow\Omega(0,\mu,\lambda)
is also analytic on the whole real line with respect to
\mu
.

Proof

i) By observing that the map
\displaystyle{\rho^{\prime}\rightarrow e^{\frac{a}{\rho^{\prime}}}}
is decreasing where
a
is an positive constant, we deduce that the map
\rho^{\prime}\rightarrow N_{\rho^{\prime}}
is also decreasing.

ii) We obtain this property from an above lemma.

iii) If we consider the integral

\displaystyle{\int_{0}^{+\infty}\int_{0}^{+\infty}N_{\rho^{\prime}}^{2}\frac{r_{\infty}(y)}{r_{\infty}(y_{1})}dydy_{1}}

then we have:

\displaystyle{\int_{0}^{+\infty}\int_{0}^{+\infty}N_{\rho^{\prime}}^{2}\frac{r_{\infty}(y)}{r_{\infty}(y_{1})}dydy_{1}}
=
\displaystyle{\int_{0}^{\rho^{\prime}}\int_{0}^{\rho^{\prime}}\tilde{N}_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,,\lambda}^{2}e^{-(y^{2}-y_{1}^{2})-2\rho(y-y_{1})}dydy_{1}}

\displaystyle{\leq C_{\rho^{\prime}}\int_{0}^{\rho^{\prime}}\int_{0}^{\rho^{\prime}}\tilde{N}_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,,\lambda}^{2}dydy_{1}}
where
C_{\rho^{\prime}}
is a constant because the function
\displaystyle{e^{-(y^{2}-y_{1}^{2})-2\rho(y-y_{1})}}
is bounded.

As
\displaystyle{\int_{0}^{\rho^{\prime}}\int_{0}^{\rho^{\prime}}\tilde{N}_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,,\lambda}^{2}dydy_{1}<+\infty}
then we deduce the property iii) of this theorem.

iv) Let
(\mathbb{X},\Sigma,\tau)
be a measure space with positive measure
\tau
. An version of the classical monotone convergence theorem of Beppo Levi formulated in terms of functions of the Lebesgue space
L_{2}(X,\Sigma,\tau)
reads as follows: If
(f_{n}:n\geq 1)
is an decreasing sequence (that is,
f_{n}(x)\geq f_{n+1}(x)
for every
n
and almost every
x
in
\mathbb{X}
) of nonnegative,square integrable functions on
\mathbb{X}
such that
\displaystyle{\int_{X}\mid f_{1}(x)\mid^{2}d\tau<+\infty}

Then

f_{n}
converges to some square integrable function
f
both almost everywhere and in
\displaystyle{L_{2}}
norm as
n\rightarrow+\infty
.

By applying this theorem, (ii) the above lemma , (i) and (ii) of theorem 3.5 we deduce that:

\displaystyle{lim\int_{0}^{+\infty}\int_{0}^{+\infty}G_{\rho^{\prime}}^{2}\frac{r_{\infty}(y)}{r_{\infty}(y_{1})}dydy_{1}}

=\displaystyle{\int_{0}^{+\infty}\int_{0}^{+\infty}N_{\mu,\lambda}^{2}\frac{r_{\infty}(y)}{r_{\infty}(y_{1})}dydy_{1}}
as
\rho^{\prime}\rightarrow+\infty

and

K_{\rho^{\prime}}
converges to
K(\mu,\lambda)
as
\rho^{\prime}
goes to infinity in Hilbert-Schmidt norm on
\displaystyle{L_{2}([0,+\infty[,r_{\infty}(y)dy)}
.

v) Let
\Omega(\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda)
be the spectral radius of
K_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}
of kernel
N_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}
and let
\Omega(0,\mu,\lambda)
be the spectral radius of
K_{0,\mu,\lambda}
of kernel
N_{0,\mu,\lambda}
then

As
N_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}
is positive decreasing sequence of kernels which converges to the positive kernel
N_{0,\mu,\lambda}
as goes to infinity we deduce that :

\Omega(\mu,\lambda)\leq Lim\quad\Omega(\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda)
as
\rho^{\prime}\rightarrow+\infty
.

Thus it suffices to show that

\displaystyle{Lim\quad\Omega(\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda)\leq\Omega(0,\mu,\lambda)}
as
\rho^{\prime}\rightarrow+\infty
.

From the above property iv) we deduce that

\forall\quad n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\displaystyle{\mid\mid K_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}^{n}\mid\mid^{\frac{1}{n}}}

\rightarrow

\displaystyle{\mid\mid K_{0,\mu,\lambda}^{n}\mid\mid^{\frac{1}{n}}}
as
\rho^{\prime}\rightarrow+\infty
.

i.e.

\displaystyle{\forall\quad\epsilon>0,\exists\quad\rho_{1};\forall\quad\rho^{\prime}\geq\rho_{1}}
we have
\displaystyle{\mid\mid K_{0,\mu,\lambda}^{n}\mid\mid^{\frac{1}{n}}-\epsilon\leq\mid\mid K_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}^{n}\mid\mid^{\frac{1}{n}}\leq\mid\mid K_{0,\mu,\lambda}^{n}\mid\mid^{\frac{1}{n}}+\epsilon}
.

set
\rho_{1}=\frac{\mu}{\lambda_{1}^{{}^{\prime}}}
then we get

\displaystyle{\mid\mid K_{\lambda_{1}^{{}^{\prime}},\mu,\lambda}^{n}\mid\mid^{\frac{1}{n}}\leq\mid\mid K_{0,\mu,\lambda}^{n}\mid\mid^{\frac{1}{n}}+\epsilon}
.

In particular, we get

\displaystyle{\Omega(\lambda_{1}^{{}^{\prime}},\mu,\lambda)\leq\Omega(0,\mu,\lambda)+\epsilon}

(3.97)

Now as

\displaystyle{\Omega(\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda)=lim\quad\mid\mid K_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}^{n}\mid\mid^{\frac{1}{n}}}
as
n\rightarrow+\infty

i.e.

\displaystyle{\forall\quad\epsilon>0,\exists\quad n_{0}\in\mathbb{N};\forall\quad n\geq n_{0}}
we have
\displaystyle{\Omega(\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda)-\epsilon\leq\mid\mid K_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}^{n}\mid\mid^{\frac{1}{n}}\leq\Omega_{(\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda)}+\epsilon}

By using the above right inequality and the decreasing of the map
\rho^{\prime}\rightarrow N_{\rho^{\prime}}
, we deduce that:

\displaystyle{\mid\mid K_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}^{n}\mid\mid^{\frac{1}{n}}\leq\Omega(\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda)+\epsilon\leq\Omega(\lambda_{1}^{{}^{\prime}},\mu,\lambda)+\epsilon}

(3.98)

and by (3.97) and (3.98) and for all
\epsilon>0
, we get:

\displaystyle{lim\quad\Omega_{\rho^{\prime}}}

\leq

\displaystyle{\Omega(0,\mu,\lambda)+2\epsilon}
as
\rho^{\prime}\rightarrow+\infty

Then

\displaystyle{lim\quad\Omega(\lambda^{{}^{\prime}},\mu,\lambda)=\Omega(0,\mu,\lambda)}
as
\lambda^{\prime}\rightarrow 0
.

vi) Let
\displaystyle{\tilde{N}_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}(y,y_{1})=\frac{1}{\lambda y_{1}\sqrt{r(y_{1})}}\int_{0}^{min(y,y_{1})}e^{-\rho^{\prime}s}(1-\frac{s}{\rho^{\prime}})^{-(\delta+1)}ds}
where

\delta=\rho^{\prime}(\rho+\rho^{\prime})-1=\rho^{\prime 2}+\rho\rho^{\prime}-1

then
\displaystyle{\tilde{N}_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}(y,y_{1})}
can be written as

\displaystyle{\tilde{N}_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}(y,y_{1})=\frac{1}{\lambda y_{1}}e^{\rho^{\prime}y_{1}}(1-\frac{y_{1}}{\rho^{\prime}})^{\rho^{\prime 2}-1}\int_{0}^{min(y,y_{1})}e^{-\rho^{\prime}s}(1-\frac{s}{\rho^{\prime}})^{-\rho^{\prime 2}}[\frac{\rho^{\prime}-y_{1}}{\rho^{\prime}-s}]^{\rho\rho^{\prime}}ds}

(3.99)

As
0\leq\leq y_{1}
, the map
\displaystyle{\mu\rightarrow[\frac{\rho^{\prime}-y_{1}}{\rho^{\prime}-s}]^{\rho\rho^{\prime}}}
is creasing with respect to
\mu
and we get vi).

vii) Let
\displaystyle{\delta_{\beta}=\rho^{\prime}(\frac{\beta}{\lambda}+\rho^{\prime})-1}
and
\displaystyle{L_{2,\beta}([0,\rho^{\prime}],r_{\beta}(y)dy)}
be the space of square integrable functions with respect to the measure
\displaystyle{r_{\beta}(y)=e^{2\rho^{\prime}y}(1-\frac{y}{\rho^{\prime}})^{2\delta_{\beta}}}
.

To use the results of chapter VII of Kato’s book
[Kato]
on the operators depending of a parameter, we shall consider the map:

\displaystyle{\mu\rightarrow<\phi,K_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}\psi>=\int_{0}^{\rho^{\prime}}\phi(-iy)\int_{0}^{\rho^{\prime}}\tilde{N}_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}(y,y_{1})\bar{\psi}(-iy_{1})dy_{1}r_{\beta}(y)dy}

(3.100)

with
\displaystyle{\phi\in L_{2,\beta}([0,\rho^{\prime}],r_{\beta}(y)dy)}
and
\displaystyle{\psi\in L_{2,\beta}([0,\rho^{\prime}],r_{\beta}(y)dy)}

We being by showing that the map defined by (3.100) is continuous with respect to
\mu
:

Let
\displaystyle{\tilde{N}_{\lambda^{\prime},\beta}}
be the expression of
\displaystyle{\tilde{N}_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}}
where we have replaced
\delta
by
\delta_{\beta}
.

Now, we set
\displaystyle{f_{\mu}(y,y_{1})=e^{2\rho^{\prime}y}(1-\frac{y}{\rho^{\prime}})^{2\delta_{\beta}}\tilde{N}_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}(y,y_{1})\phi(-iy)\bar{\psi}(-iy_{1})}
then for
\mu\geq\beta
we have:

\displaystyle{\mid f_{\mu}(y,y_{1})\mid=e^{2\rho^{\prime}y}(1-\frac{y}{\rho^{\prime}})^{2\delta_{\beta}}\tilde{N}_{\lambda^{\prime},\beta}(y,y_{1})\mid\phi(-iy)\mid\mid\bar{\psi}(-iy_{1})\mid}

then the continuity of (3.100) with respect to
\mu
follows by applying the bounded convergence theorem.

Now to show that the map defined by (3.100) is analytic with respect to
\mu
, it suffices to observe that for all closed curve we have

\displaystyle{\int_{\gamma}\tilde{N}_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}d\mu=0}

(3.101)

and to apply the following theorem:

Theorem 3.34
.

(Reed-Simon
[Reed et al]
, theorem XII.8)

Let
\mathbb{E}
be a complex Banach space and
L(\mathbb{E})
be the set of bounded operators on
\mathbb{E}
with respect to the operator norm.

Let
\mathbb{T}
be an analytic function
(\mu\in\mathbb{C}\rightarrow\mathbb{T}(\mu)\in L(\mathbb{E}))
and
\sigma_{0}
be a simple eigenvalue of
T(\mu_{0})
.Then

There exists an analytic function
\sigma
of
\mu
for
\mu
near
\mu_{0}
such that
\sigma(\mu)
is simple eigenvalue of
\mathbb{T}(\mu)
and
\sigma(\mu_{0})=\sigma_{0}
.
\square

We conclude this section by noting that for
\lambda^{\prime}\neq 0
, the operator
H_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}
has a rich set of spectral properties desired by physicists and for
\lambda^{\prime}=0
, the results of Ando-Zerner were a major contribution in the spectral study of the operator
H_{0,\mu,\lambda}
, however the density of its eigenvectors in Bargmann space is always open question.

4
Study of the singularity of
\displaystyle{H_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}\varphi(z)=\sigma(\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda)\varphi(z)\,at\,\infty}

4.1
On bi-confluent Heun equation associated to eigenvalue problem of
\displaystyle{H_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}}

We consider the equation

\displaystyle{(\lambda^{\prime}z^{2}+i\lambda z)\varphi^{{}^{\prime\prime}}(z)+(i\lambda z^{2}+\mu z)\varphi^{{}^{\prime}}(z)=\sigma(\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda)\varphi(z)}
at
\infty
(
\star
)

In our differential equation (
\star
), if we put
\psi(z)=\varphi^{{}^{\prime}}(z)
then we get

\displaystyle{\psi^{{}^{\prime}}(z)=\frac{\sigma(\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda)}{z(\lambda^{\prime}z+i\lambda)}\varphi(z)-\frac{i\lambda+\mu}{\lambda^{\prime}z+i\lambda}\psi(z)}

(4.1)

Now let
\phi(z)=\left(\begin{array}[c]{l}\varphi(z)\\
\\
\psi(z)\\
\end{array}\right)
then

\phi^{{}^{\prime}}(z)=\left(\begin{matrix}\displaystyle{0}&\displaystyle{1}\\
\quad\\
\displaystyle{\frac{\sigma(\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda)}{z(\lambda^{\prime}z+i\lambda)}}&\displaystyle{-\frac{i\lambda+\mu}{\lambda^{\prime}z+i\lambda}}\\
\end{matrix}\right)\left(\begin{array}[c]{l}\varphi(z)\\
\\
\psi(z)\\
\end{array}\right)

(4.2)

If we put
\displaystyle{z=re^{i\theta};r\in[0,+\infty[,\theta\in[0,2\pi]}
and
W(r)=\left(\begin{array}[c]{l}\varphi(re^{i\theta})\\
\\
\psi(re^{i\theta})\\
\end{array}\right)
, we deduce that

\displaystyle{W^{\prime}(r)=e^{-i\theta}\mathbb{M}(re^{i\theta})W(r)}

(4.3)

where
\mathbb{M}(re^{i\theta})=\left(\begin{matrix}\displaystyle{0}&\displaystyle{1}\\
\quad\\
\displaystyle{\frac{\sigma(\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda)}{re^{i\theta}(\lambda^{\prime}re^{i\theta}+i\lambda)}}&\displaystyle{-\frac{i\lambda+\mu}{\lambda^{\prime}re^{i\theta}+i\lambda}}\\
\end{matrix}\right)

In
\mathbb{C}^{2}
we denote the norm of
W
by
\mid\mid W\mid\mid
and the norm of matrix
\mathbb{M}
by
\mid\mid\mid\mathbb{M}\mid\mid\mid
.

As
\displaystyle{\mid\mid\mathbb{M}(re^{i\theta})W(re^{i\theta})\mid\mid\,\leq\,\mid\mid\mid\mathbb{M}\mid\mid\mid\,\mid\mid W\mid\mid}
, then we deduce that

\displaystyle{\mid\mid\mathbb{M}^{{}^{\prime}}(r)\leq\mid\mid\mid\mathbb{M}\mid\mid\mid\,\mid\mid W(r)\mid\mid}

(4.4)

Let
R>\rho^{\prime}
then the norm
\mid\mid\mid\mathbb{M}\mid\mid\mid
is bounded outside the disk of radius
R
and by applying the Gronwal Lemma, we deduce the following inequality

\displaystyle{\forall\,r\in[R,+\infty[,\mid\mid\phi(z)\mid\mid\leq sup_{\theta\in[0,2\pi]}\mid\mid W(Re^{i\theta})\mid\mid e^{\mid\mid\mid\mathbb{M}\mid\mid\mid(\mid z\mid-R)}}

(4.5)

In particular, we get

\displaystyle{\mid\varphi(z)\mid\leq sup_{\theta\in[0,2\pi]}\mid\mid W(Re^{i\theta})\mid\mid e^{\mid\mid\mid\mathbb{M}\mid\mid\mid(\mid z\mid-R)}}

(4.6)

Therefore
\varphi
belongs to Bargmann space
\mathbb{B}_{0}

4.2
On eigenvalues of
H_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}
acting on
\displaystyle{L_{2}((0,\rho^{\prime}),r(y)dy)}

Let
\rho^{\prime}>0,y\in[0,\rho^{\prime}]
and
\displaystyle{L_{2}((0,\rho^{\prime}),r(y)dy)}
be the Hilbert space of square integrable functions on
[0,\rho^{\prime}]
with inner
product

\displaystyle{<u,v>=\int_{0}^{\rho^{\prime}}u(y)\overline{v(y)}r(y)dy}

(4.7)

with
r(y)
called weight function.

We will construct the fonction
r(y)
such that
H_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}
is symmetric on
\displaystyle{L_{2}((0,\rho^{\prime}),r(y)dy)}
.

For
\varphi\in\mathbb{B}_{0}
, consider
u(y)=\varphi(-iy)
and the eigenvalue problem associated to
H_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}
:

\displaystyle{-y(\lambda-\lambda^{\prime}y)u^{"}(y)+y(\lambda y+\mu y)u^{\prime}(y)=\sigma(\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda)u(y)}

(4.8)

By multiplying the differential equation (4.8) by
r(y)\overline{u(y)}
and integrating it on
[0,\rho^{\prime}]
to get

\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\displaystyle{\int_{0}^{\rho^{\prime}}[-y(\lambda-\lambda^{\prime}y)u^{"}(y)+y(\lambda y+\mu y)u^{\prime}(y)]\overline{u(y)}r(y)dy=}\\
\displaystyle{\sigma(\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda)\int_{0}^{\rho^{\prime}}\mid u(y)\mid^{2}r(y)dy\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad}\end{array}\right.

(4.9)

Integrating the first term of integral equation by parts to get

\displaystyle{[s(y)\overline{u(y)}u^{\prime}(y)(\lambda^{\prime}y-\lambda)]_{0}^{\rho^{\prime}}-\int_{0}^{\rho^{\prime}}s(y)(\lambda^{\prime}y-\lambda)\mid u^{\prime}(y)\mid^{2}dy}

+
\displaystyle{\int_{0}^{\rho^{\prime}}[(\lambda y+\mu)s(y)-\frac{\partial}{\partial y}((\lambda^{\prime}y-\lambda)s(y))]\mid u(y)\mid^{2}dy}

\displaystyle{=\sigma(\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda)\int_{0}^{\rho^{\prime}}\mid u(y)\mid^{2}r(y)dy}

(4.10)

where
s(y)=yr(y)

We choose
s(y)
such that

\displaystyle{\frac{\partial}{\partial y}((\lambda^{\prime}y-\lambda)s(y))=(\lambda y+\mu)s(y)}

(4.11)

Then

\displaystyle{s(y)=ce^{\rho^{\prime}y}\mid y-\rho^{\prime}\mid^{\rho^{\prime}(\rho+\rho^{\prime})-1}}\,and\,\displaystyle{r(y)=\frac{s(y)}{y}},c\,is\,const.

(4.12)

Theorem 4.1
.

if
\displaystyle{\rho^{\prime}(\rho+\rho^{\prime})\geq 1}
then
H_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}
is symmetric on
\displaystyle{L_{2}((0,\rho^{\prime}),r(y)dy)}
.

Proof

if
\displaystyle{\rho^{\prime}(\rho+\rho^{\prime})\geq 1}
then the function
s(y)
is bounded on
[0,\rho^{\prime}]
this implies that

\displaystyle{[s(y)\overline{u(y)}u^{\prime}(y)(\lambda^{\prime}y-\lambda)]_{0}^{\rho^{\prime}}=0}

(4.13)

Consequently

if
\displaystyle{\rho^{\prime}(\rho+\rho^{\prime})\geq 1}
then the function
s(y)
is bounded on
[0,\rho^{\prime}]
this implies that

\displaystyle{-\int_{0}^{\rho^{\prime}}s(y)(\lambda^{\prime}y-\lambda)\mid u^{\prime}(y)\mid^{2}dy}=\displaystyle{\sigma(\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda)\int_{0}^{\rho^{\prime}}\mid u(y)\mid^{2}r(y)dy}

(4.14)

i.e
H_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}
is symmetric on
\displaystyle{L_{2}((0,\rho^{\prime}),r(y)dy)}
.
\square

Corollary 4.2
.

(i) if
\displaystyle{\rho^{\prime}(\rho+\rho^{\prime})\geq 1}
then the the eigenvalues of
H_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}
are real.

(ii) On
\displaystyle{L_{2}((0,\rho^{\prime}),r(y)dy)}
, the operator
H_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}
takes the following form

if
\displaystyle{\rho^{\prime}(\rho+\rho^{\prime})\geq 1}
then the function
s(y)
is bounded on
[0,\rho^{\prime}]
this implies that

\displaystyle{H_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}u(y)=\frac{1}{r(y)}\frac{\partial}{\partial y}(p(y)u^{\prime}(y))}

(4.15)

where
\displaystyle{p(y)=\lambda^{\prime}y(\rho^{\prime}-y)r(y)}
.

Proof

(i) It is a simple consequence of the above theorem and the fact that the space of the restrictions of the elements of the domain of the operator
H_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}
as operator acting on Bargmann’s space
\mathbb{B}_{0}
is included in

\displaystyle{\mathbb{B}_{2}=\{u\in L_{2}((0,\rho^{\prime}),r(y)dy);H_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}u\in L_{2}((0,\rho^{\prime}),r(y)dy);u(0)=0\}}

(ii) It is a simple consequence.
\square

Proposition 4.3
.

if
\displaystyle{\rho^{\prime}(\rho+\rho^{\prime})\geq 1}
then the eigenvalues of
H_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}
acting on
\displaystyle{L_{2}((0,\rho^{\prime}),r(y)dy)}
are simple.

Proof

Let
u,v\in\displaystyle{\mathbb{B}_{2}}
then
\displaystyle{vH_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}u=\frac{v}{r(y)}\frac{\partial}{\partial y}(p(y)u^{\prime}(y))}
,
\displaystyle{uH_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}v=\frac{u}{r(y)}\frac{\partial}{\partial y}(p(y)v^{\prime}(y))}

and

if
\displaystyle{\rho^{\prime}(\rho+\rho^{\prime})\geq 1}
then the function
s(y)
is bounded on
[0,\rho^{\prime}]
this implies that

\displaystyle{r(y)(vH_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}u-uH_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}v=\frac{\partial}{\partial y}[p(y)(v(y)u^{\prime}(y)-u(y)v^{\prime}(y)]}

(4.16)

If
\displaystyle{H_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}u(y)=\sigma(\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda)u(y);u\neq 0}
and
\displaystyle{H_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}v(y)=\sigma(\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda)v(y);v\neq 0}
then

\displaystyle{\frac{\partial}{\partial y}[p(y)(v(y)u^{\prime}(y)-u(y)v^{\prime}(y)]=0}
, in particular
p(y)(v(y)u^{\prime}(y)-u(y)v^{\prime}(y)
is a constant.

As
p(0)((v(0)u^{\prime}(0)-u(0)v^{\prime}(0))=0
then
v(y)u^{\prime}(y)-u(y)v^{\prime}(y)=0\,\forall\,y\in[0,\rho^{\prime}]
and
v
is proportional to
u
i.e. the geometric multiplicity of
\sigma(\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda)
is one.

Now, let
\displaystyle{Ker(H_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}-\sigma(\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda)I)=\{u\in\mathbb{B}_{2};H_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}u=\sigma(\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda)u;u\neq 0\}}
, showing that there are no Jordan blocks corresponding to
\sigma(\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda)
i.e;

\displaystyle{Ker(H_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}-\sigma(\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda)I)=Ker(H_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}-\sigma(\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda)I)^{2}}

,\displaystyle{\forall\,\sigma(\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda)\in\sigma(H_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda})}

where
\displaystyle{\sigma(H_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda})}
denotes the spectrum of
\displaystyle{H_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}}
.

Let
\displaystyle{u\in Ker(H_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}-\sigma(\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda)I)}
and
\displaystyle{v\in Ker(H_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}-\sigma(\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda)I)^{2}}
such that if
\displaystyle{\rho^{\prime}(\rho+\rho^{\prime})\geq 1}
then the function
s(y)
is bounded on
[0,\rho^{\prime}]
this implies that

\displaystyle{\frac{1}{r(y)}\frac{\partial}{\partial y}(p(y)u^{\prime}(y))u(y)=\sigma(\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda)u(y),\quad y\in[0,\rho^{\prime}]}

(4.17)

and

if
\displaystyle{\rho^{\prime}(\rho+\rho^{\prime})\geq 1}
then the function
s(y)
is bounded on
[0,\rho^{\prime}]
this implies that

\displaystyle{\frac{1}{r(y)}\frac{\partial}{\partial y}(p(y)v^{\prime}(y))v(y)=\sigma(\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda)v(y)+u(y),\quad y\in[0,\rho^{\prime}]}

(4.18)

Multiplyng the differential equation by
r(y)\overline{v(y)}
and by
r(y)\overline{v(y)}
, integrating by parts the obtained equations on
[0,\rho^{\prime}]
to get

if
\displaystyle{\rho^{\prime}(\rho+\rho^{\prime})\geq 1}
then the function
s(y)
is bounded on
[0,\rho^{\prime}]
this implies that

\displaystyle{\int_{0}^{\rho^{\prime}}p(y)u^{\prime}(y)\overline{v}^{\prime}(y)dy=\sigma(\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda)\int_{0}^{\rho^{\prime}}r(y)u(y)\overline{v(y)}dy}

(4.19)

and

if
\displaystyle{\rho^{\prime}(\rho+\rho^{\prime})\geq 1}
then the function
s(y)
is bounded on
[0,\rho^{\prime}]
this implies that

\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\displaystyle{\int_{0}^{\rho^{\prime}}p(y)v^{\prime}(y)\overline{u}^{\prime}(y)dy=\sigma(\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda)\int_{0}^{\rho^{\prime}}r(y)v(y)\overline{u(y)}dy\quad+}\\
\displaystyle{\int_{0}^{\rho^{\prime}}r(y)\mid u(y)\mid^{2}dy\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad}\end{array}\right.

(4.20)

Now (4.19) - (4.20) gives

\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\displaystyle{\int_{0}^{\rho^{\prime}}p(y)[u^{\prime}(y)\overline{v}^{\prime}(y)-v^{\prime}(y)\overline{u}^{\prime}(y)]dy=\quad\quad}\\
\displaystyle{\sigma(\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda)\int_{0}^{\rho^{\prime}}r(y)[u(y)\overline{v(y)}-v(y)\overline{u(y)}]dy}\\
\displaystyle{-\int_{0}^{\rho^{\prime}}r(y)\mid u(y)\mid^{2}dy}\end{array}\right.

(4.21)

In particular we have

\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\displaystyle{2i\int_{0}^{\rho^{\prime}}p(y)\Im m(u^{\prime}(y)\overline{v}^{\prime}(y))dy=}\\
\displaystyle{2i\sigma(\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda)\int_{0}^{\rho^{\prime}}r(y)\Im m(u(y)\overline{v(y)})-v(y)dy}\\
\displaystyle{-\int_{0}^{\rho^{\prime}}r(y)\mid u(y)\mid^{2}dy}\end{array}\right.

(4.22)

As
p
is real function and
\sigma(\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda)\in\mathbb{R}
then
\displaystyle{\int_{0}^{\rho^{\prime}}r(y)\mid u(y)\mid^{2}dy=0}
which is contradictory with
u
is eigenfunction associated to
\sigma(\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda)
.
\square

We end these first spectral properties on
H_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}
by the following observations

(1) The hamiltonian
H_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}
acting on Bargmann space (which is not selfadjoint) has a real spectrum.

(2) The PT-symmetry condition (space-time reflection) is not sufficient to provide that the spectrum of
H_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}
is real, there are some PT-symmetric hamiltonian with polynomial potentials producing non real eigenvalues, see
[Delabeare et al]
.

(3) The method used by
[Dorey et al]
to give a rigorous proof of reality and positivity of the eigenvalues of some non-self-adjoint hamiltonian is not applicable to
H_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}
and even less to
H_{\mu,\lambda}

5
Review some fondamental spectral properties of
H_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}

We now turn to review the fondamental spectral properties of
H_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}
that we will presented in above sections.

\bullet
We have restricted
H_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}
to the subspace of Bargmann space consisting of the functions that vanish at
0
. Thus we get rid of the vacuum state.

A central role is played by the following inequality (for
\mu>0
):

\displaystyle{\Re e<H_{\lambda,\mu}\varphi,\varphi>=\mu\mid\mid A\varphi\mid\mid^{2}\geq\mid\mid\varphi\mid\mid^{2},\,\forall\,\varphi\in D(H_{\lambda,\mu})}

(5.1)

which obviously holds for polynomials (
\Re e
means the real part).

\bullet
Now if
\varphi
is in Bargmann space,
H_{\lambda,\mu}\varphi
can be defined as an analytic function, so that it makes sense to take as domain of
H_{\lambda,\mu}
the set of functions
\varphi
such that
H_{\lambda,\mu}\varphi
still is in Bargmann space. It turns out that so defined,
H_{\lambda,\mu}

is the closure of
H_{\lambda,\mu_{\mid_{pol}}}
, has a compact inverse and
e^{-tH_{\lambda,\mu}}
can be defined for positive
t
(see another section of this work for detailed proofs).

\bullet
Above, a rigorous mathematical analysis of domains of
H_{\mu,\lambda}
is given , in particular, we have :

\displaystyle{D(H_{\mu,\lambda})=D(H_{\mu,\lambda}^{min})}

(5.2)

where
H_{\mu,\lambda}^{min}
is the restriction of the closure of
H_{\mu,\lambda}
on the polynomials.

\bullet
By using the following inequality :

\displaystyle{\forall\,\epsilon>0,\exists\,C_{\epsilon,\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}>0;\mid\mid H_{\mu,\lambda}\varphi\mid\mid^{2}\leq\epsilon\mid\mid A^{*^{2}}A^{2}\varphi\mid\mid+C_{\epsilon,\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}\mid\mid\varphi\mid\mid\,\forall\,\varphi\in D(A^{*^{2}}A^{2})}

we can show that for
\lambda^{\prime}\neq 0
, we have

\displaystyle{D(H_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda})=D(A^{*^{2}}A^{2})}

(5.3)

\bullet
Note that the domain of the adjoint and anti-adjoint parts of
H_{\mu,\lambda}
are not included in each other, nor is the domain of their commutator.

\bullet
The restriction of the closure of the anti-part
H_{I}=A^{*}(A+A^{*})A
of
H_{\mu,\lambda}
or of
H_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}
on the polynomials is symmetric but it is not self-adjoint,
D(H_{I})\neq D(H_{I}^{min})
(where
H_{I}^{min}
is the restriction of the closure of
H_{I}
on the polynomials) and that
H_{I}
is chaotic in Devaney’s sense. In particular, the spectrum of
H_{I}
is all
\mathbb{C}
.

\bullet
When we want to proof that the eigenvalues of
H_{\mu,\lambda}
are real we define :

\displaystyle{H_{\lambda^{\prime}}:=H_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}=\lambda^{\prime}A^{*^{2}}A^{2}+H_{\mu,\lambda}}

For positive
\lambda^{\prime}
and
\mu>0
we have,

\displaystyle{\Re e<H_{\lambda^{\prime}}\varphi,\varphi>\geq\mu\mid\mid A\varphi\mid\mid^{2},\,\forall\,\varphi\in D(H_{\lambda^{\prime}})}

(5.4)

and
H_{\lambda^{\prime}}
, which is also invertible satisfies:

\displaystyle{\mid\mid AH_{\lambda^{\prime}}^{-1}\psi\mid\mid\leq\frac{1}{\mu}\mid\mid\psi\mid\mid}

(5.5)

Now the set of
\phi
satisfying
\displaystyle{\mid\mid A\phi\mid\mid^{2}\leq 1}
is a compact set and we see that all
H_{\lambda^{\prime}}
, map the unit ball into one compact set.

This enables us to prove that
\displaystyle{lim\,H_{\lambda^{\prime}}^{-1}=H_{\mu,\lambda}^{-1}\,as\,\lambda^{\prime}\longrightarrow 0}
in the sense of the norm of operators and this implies the convergence of the eigenvalues.

(v) The operator
H_{\mu,\lambda}
respectively
H_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}
has the form
\displaystyle{p(z)\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial z^{2}}+q(z))\frac{\partial}{\partial z}}
with
p(z)=i\lambda z
(respectively
p(z)=\lambda^{\prime}z^{2}+i\lambda z
) and
q(z)=i\lambda z^{2}+\mu z
of degree
2
and
H_{\mu,\lambda}
and
H_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}
are of Heun operator type respectively.

(vi) The eigenvalue problem associated to
H_{\mu,\lambda}
(respectively
H_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}
) does not satisfy the classical ordinary differential equation of the form:

\displaystyle{\sigma(z)\frac{\partial^{2}\varphi}{\partial z^{2}}+\tau(z))\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial z}=\alpha\varphi}

(5.6)

where
\sigma(z)
is a polynomial of degree at most two,
\tau(z)
is a polynomial of the degree exactly one and
\alpha
is a constant.

(vii) If
\lambda\neq 0
, the eigenvalue problem associated to
H_{\mu,\lambda}
can be written as follows :

\displaystyle{z\frac{\partial^{2}\varphi}{\partial z^{2}}+(z^{2}-i\rho z)\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial z}=-i\frac{\alpha}{\lambda}\varphi;\rho=\frac{\mu}{\lambda}}

(5.7)

Let
z=i\sqrt{2}\xi
and
\varphi(\sqrt{2}\xi)=\psi(\xi)
then (5.7) can be transformed to :

\displaystyle{\xi\frac{\partial^{2}\psi}{\partial\xi^{2}}+(-2\xi^{2}+\rho\sqrt{2}\xi)\frac{\partial\psi}{\partial\xi}-\frac{\alpha\sqrt{2}}{\lambda}\psi=0}

(5.8)

(viii) If
\lambda^{\prime}\neq 0
and let
z=-i\rho^{\prime}\xi
and
\varphi(z)=\psi(\xi)
then the eigenvalue problem associated to
H_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}
can be transformed to follows :

\displaystyle{\xi(\xi-1)\frac{\partial^{2}\psi}{\partial\xi^{2}}+(\rho^{\prime 2}\xi^{2}+\rho^{\prime\prime}\xi)\frac{\partial\psi}{\partial\psi}=\frac{\alpha}{\lambda^{\prime}}\psi;\rho^{\prime}=\frac{\lambda}{\lambda^{\prime}},\rho^{\prime\prime}=\frac{\mu}{\lambda^{\prime}}}

(5.9)

(ix) Our equation belongs to family of the bi-confluent Heun equations of class
(0,1,1_{4})

\displaystyle{H_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta}u(z)=zu^{\prime\prime}(z)+(-2z^{2}-\beta z+1+\alpha)u^{\prime}(z)+[(\gamma-\alpha-2)z-\delta]u(z)=0}

(5.10)

where
\displaystyle{\delta=\frac{1}{2}(\hat{\delta}+\beta(1+\alpha))}

which have one regular singular points
z_{0}=0
and one irregular singular point
z_{\infty}=\infty
.

This equation belongs to family of confluent Heun equations (CHE)

\displaystyle{H_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta,\epsilon}u(z)=z(z-1)u^{\prime\prime}(z)+(\alpha z^{2}+\beta z+\gamma)u^{\prime}(z)+(\delta z+\epsilon)u(z)=0}

(5.11)

which has two regular singular points
z_{0}=0
and
z_{1}=1
and one irregular singular point
z_{\infty}=\infty
.

These equations are more complicated than the equations for classical special functions such as Bessel functions, hypergeometric functions, and confluent hypergeometric functions (see
[Betman et al.]
,
[Choun: [1-10)]]
) and
[Abramovitz]
).

Above equation includes, as a special (or confluent) case, the Mathieu equation, the equation for Coulomb spheroidal functions, and many other equations used in mathematical physics and applied mathematics. However, it cannot be solved in closed integral form, and analytic tools for investigation of its solutions are rather poor.

It turns out that integral relations are useful for analytic studies of equation and similar objects. Integral relations for equations of the Heun class were discussed in
[Erdelyi]
and
[Kasakov1 et al.]
. In
[Kasakov2 et al.]
such integral relations were used in order to obtain Fredholm integral equations for eigenfunctions of the boundary problems associated with equations of the Heun class and in
[Kasakov1 et al.]
, integral relations between the eigenfunctions of different equations were established.

The aim result of this work is to specify the boundary conditions of Schrodinger equations associated to “Gribov-Intissar” operators and to give an analysis of behavior of scattering amplitudes with respect the parameters.

As
H_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}
and
H_{\mu,\lambda}
are non-self-adjoint unbounded operators and that the space

\displaystyle{\mathbb{B}_{0}=\{\varphi\in\mathbb{B};\varphi(0)=0\}}
is invariant under their actions then the first idea is to show that their respectively resolvent sets are non empty and to show that their respectively resolvent is compact.

In
[Intissar2]
, it was showed that
H_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}^{-1}
and
H_{\mu,\lambda}^{-1}
exist and they are compact using the compactness of the injection of their respective domains into Bargmann space and that their respective resolvent sets are non-empty.

Let
\psi\in\mathbb{B}_{0}
and
\varphi\in D(H_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda})
(respectively
\varphi\in D(H_{\mu,\lambda})
), we consider the equation
H_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}\varphi=\psi
(respectively
H_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}\varphi=\psi
) which can be written under following form :

\displaystyle{(\lambda^{\prime}z^{2}+i\lambda z)\varphi^{\prime\prime}(z)+(i\lambda z^{2}+\mu z)\varphi^{\prime}(z)=\psi(z)}

(5.12)

respectively :

\displaystyle{i\lambda z\varphi^{\prime\prime}(z)+(i\lambda z^{2}+\mu z)\varphi^{\prime}(z)=\psi(z)}

(5.13)

Let
\psi\in\mathbb{B}_{0}
and choosing the straight line connecting
-i\rho^{\prime},z\in\mathbb{C}
parametrized by

\displaystyle{\gamma:[0,1]\rightarrow\mathbb{C},\gamma(t)=-i\rho^{\prime}+t(z+i\rho^{\prime})\quad\quad(\gamma(0)=-i\rho^{\prime},\gamma(1)=z)}

If we define
\displaystyle{\int_{-i\rho^{\prime}}^{z}\psi(\xi)d\xi:=\int_{\gamma}\psi(\xi)d\xi:=\int_{0}^{1}}\psi(\gamma(t))\gamma^{\prime}(t)dt
then our equation can be transformed to following integral equation :

\displaystyle{\varphi(z)=\frac{1}{\lambda^{\prime}}\int_{-i\rho^{\prime}}^{z}e^{-i\rho^{\prime}\eta}(\eta+i\rho^{\prime})^{-(\delta+1)}[\int_{-i\rho^{\prime}}^{\eta}e^{i\rho^{\prime}\xi}(\xi+i\rho^{\prime})^{\delta}\frac{\psi(\xi)}{\xi}d\xi]d\eta}

(5.14)

In the same way we get on
]-i\infty,0]
an integral equation with
\lambda^{\prime}=0

However, the integral representation of
\varphi(z)
in the equations is hard to study in
\mathbb{C}
for some existing results on eigenvalues and eigenfunctions of our operators. To overcome this difficulty, the study of integral equations associated to our equations are restricted on negative imaginary axis.Therefore led to put
\psi(y)=\varphi(-iy)
;
y\in[0,+\infty[
to study the following problems :

\displaystyle{-\lambda y\psi_{\mu,\lambda}^{{}^{\prime\prime}}(y)+(\lambda y^{2}+\mu y)\psi_{\mu,\lambda}^{{}^{\prime}}(y)=\sigma(\mu,\lambda)\psi_{\mu,\lambda}(y)}

(5.15)

\displaystyle{(\lambda^{\prime}y^{2}-\lambda y)\psi_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}^{"}(y)+(\lambda y^{2}+\mu y)\psi_{\mu,\lambda}^{{}^{\prime}}(y)=\sigma(\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda)\psi_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}(y)}

(5.16)

On

\displaystyle{\mathbb{B}_{1}=\{\psi:[0,\infty[\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}\,entire\,;\int_{0}^{\infty}\mid\psi(y)\mid^{2}e^{-\mid y\mid^{2}}dy<\infty\}}

(5.17)

We note that above equations are bi-confluent Heun equations and we observe that
(5.17)
possesses the origin as regular singular point and
\infty
as irregular singular point but
(5.17)
possesses
y=0
and
y=\displaystyle{\rho^{\prime}=\frac{\lambda}{\lambda^{\prime}}}
as regular singular points and
\infty
as irregular singular point.

On negative imaginary axis an explicit inverse of
H_{\mu,\lambda}
(respectively of
H_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}
) is given by :

\bullet
For
y\in[0,+\infty[
, we get:

\displaystyle{H_{\mu,\lambda}^{-1}\varphi(-iy)=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathcal{N}_{\mu,\lambda}(y,s)\varphi(-is)ds}

(5.18)

where
\displaystyle{\mathcal{N}_{\mu,\lambda}(y,s)=\frac{1}{s}e^{-\frac{s^{2}}{2}-\frac{\mu}{\lambda}s}\int_{0}^{min(y,s)}e^{\frac{u^{2}}{2}+\frac{\mu}{\lambda}u}du}

\bullet
The boundary conditions of the problem are given by:

\displaystyle{\frac{\partial u_{\mu,\lambda}}{\partial t}(t,y)-\lambda y\frac{\partial^{2}u_{\mu,\lambda}}{\partial y^{2}}(t,y)+(\lambda y^{2}+\mu y)\frac{\partial u_{\mu,\lambda}}{\partial y}(t,y)=0}

(5.19)

are

\displaystyle{u_{\mu,\lambda}(t,0)=0}\,and\,\displaystyle{lim\,e^{-\frac{1}{2}(y-\frac{\mu}{\lambda})^{2}}\frac{\partial u_{\mu,\lambda}}{\partial y}(t,y)=0}

(5.20)

as
y\longrightarrow+\infty
for all
t

\bullet
For
y\in[0,\rho^{{}^{\prime}}]
,
\displaystyle{\rho^{\prime}=\frac{\lambda}{\lambda^{\prime}}}
and
\displaystyle{\rho=\frac{\mu}{\lambda}}
, we get:

\displaystyle{H_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}^{-1}\varphi(-iy)=\int_{0}^{\rho^{\prime}}\mathcal{N}_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}(y,s)\varphi(-is)ds}

(5.21)

where
\displaystyle{\mathcal{N}_{\mu,\lambda}(y,s)=\frac{1}{\lambda s}e^{\rho^{\prime}s}(1-\frac{s}{\rho^{\prime}})^{\delta}\int_{0}^{min(y,s)}e^{-\rho^{\prime}u}(1-\frac{u}{\rho^{\prime}})^{-(\rho+1)}du}

And we have the following properties

(i) The boundary conditions on regular singular points
y=0
and
y=\rho^{\prime}
of the problem :

\displaystyle{\frac{\partial u_{\mu,\lambda}}{\partial t}(t,y)+(\lambda^{\prime}y^{2}-\lambda y)\psi_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}^{"}(y)+(\lambda y^{2}+\mu y)\psi_{\mu,\lambda}^{{}^{\prime}}(y)=0}

(5.22)

are

\displaystyle{u_{\mu,\lambda}(t,0)=0}\mbox{ and }\displaystyle{u_{\mu,\lambda}(t,y)}\mbox{\,is\, an\, analytic\, function\, around\, }y=\rho^{\prime}

(5.23)

(ii) The space of the restrictions of the elements of the domain of the operator
H_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}
is included in

\displaystyle{\mathbb{B}_{2}=\{u\in L_{2}([0,\rho^{\prime}],r(y)dy);H_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}u\in L_{2}([0,\rho^{\prime}],r(y)dy),u(0)=0\}}

(5.24)

where the expression of
H_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}u
is given by

\displaystyle{H_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}u=-\frac{1}{r(y)}\frac{\partial}{\partial y}[-\lambda^{\prime}y(\rho^{\prime}-y)r(y)\frac{\partial u}{\partial y}]}\,with\,\displaystyle{r(y)=\frac{1}{y}e^{\rho^{\prime}y}\mid y-\rho^{\prime}\mid^{\rho^{\prime}(\rho^{\prime}+\rho)-1}}

(5.25)

(iii)
\displaystyle{H_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}}
acting on
L_{2}([0,\rho^{\prime}],r(y)dy)
with domain :

\displaystyle{\mathbb{B}_{2}=\{u\in L_{2}([0,\rho^{\prime}],r(y)dy);H_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}u\in L_{2}([0,\rho^{\prime}],r(y)dy),u(0)=0\}}

is symmetric.

We will show that
H_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}
has one real eigenvector
\varphi_{0}\in\mathbb{B}_{2}
such that
\varphi_{0}\geq 0
(almost everywhere with respect to
r(y)dy
on
[0,\rho^{\prime}]
).

If
\sigma_{0}
stands for the eigenvalue of
H_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}
associated with
\varphi_{0}
, the symmetry of
H_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}
implies that, for all
\varphi\in\mathbb{B}_{2}
we have
\displaystyle{\Re e[(H_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}-\sigma_{0}I)\varphi\geq 0}
. The last results motivates the following definition:

Definition 5.1
.

(Local energy)

For any
\varphi\in\mathbb{B}_{2}
, the local energy is the function
\mathcal{E}:[0,\rho^{\prime}]\longrightarrow\overline{\mathbb{R}}
defined by :

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}_{\varphi}(y)=\frac{\Re e(H_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}\varphi(y))}{\Re e(\varphi(y))}}
.

Therefore from what precedes, we get

Let
\sigma_{0}
be the smallest eigenvalue of
H_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}
then we have

(
\alpha
) For
\forall

\varphi\in\mathbb{B}_{2}
such that
\displaystyle{\Re e(\varphi)\geq 0,inf_{[0,\rho^{\prime}]}(\mathcal{E}_{\varphi})\leq\sigma_{0}\leq sup_{[0,\rho^{\prime}]}(\mathcal{E}_{\varphi})}
.

From above property, we deduce that:

(
\beta
) Let
\sigma_{0}
be the smallest eigenvalue of
H_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}
acting on Bargmann space then we have for

\forall

\varphi\in D(H_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}^{*})
such that
\Re e(\varphi)\geq 0
,
\displaystyle{inf_{[0,\rho^{\prime}]}\frac{[\Re e(H_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}^{*}\varphi)}{\varphi}]\leq\sigma_{0}\leq sup_{[0,\rho^{\prime}]}\frac{[\Re e(H_{\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}^{*}\varphi)}{\varphi}]}
.

By using Kato’s inequalities, Thirring (1979) in
[Thirring]
have obtained the following theorem on Schrodinger operator acting on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
(for this topic see also
[Duffin]
(1947),
[Barnsley]
(1978) and
[Schmutz]
(1985))

Theorem 5.2
.

(Thirring)

Let
\mathbb{H}=-\Delta+\mathbb{V}
be a Schrodinger operator acting on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
having an eigenvalue
below
the essential spectrum. Then the lowest eigenvalue
\epsilon_{0}
of
\mathbb{H}
is such that for any strictly positive
\varphi\in D(\mathbb{H})
,

\displaystyle{inf_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}(\mathbb{V}-\frac{\Delta\varphi}{\varphi})\leq\epsilon_{0}\leq sup_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}(\mathbb{V}-\frac{\Delta\varphi}{\varphi})}
.

In Gribov’s Reggeon calculus, the Regge-poles are not the only singularities of the amplitude. There are also branch points which correspond to the exchange of several Reggeons. A Regge pole can be interpreted as corresponding to a single scattering. Regge cuts-multiple scatterings of hardons’
s
constituents.

We recall that the transition of a closed system of particles from an initial state
\mid\varphi>
to a final state
\mid\psi>
is described in quantum theory by the
\mathbb{S}
matrix (called scattering matrix)
\displaystyle{|\psi>=\mathbb{S}|\phi>}
and the matrix elements of the
\mathbb{S}

\displaystyle{\mathbb{S}_{\psi,\varphi}=<\psi\mid\mathbb{S}\mid\varphi>}
can be represented in the form:

\displaystyle{\mathbb{S}_{\psi,\varphi}=\delta_{\psi,\varphi}+C\mathbb{T}_{\psi,\varphi}}

(5.26)

where
\displaystyle{\delta_{\psi,\varphi}=1}
if the state does not change
(\mid\psi>=\mid\varphi>)
i.e. no interaction,
C
denote the conservation of
energy and momentum and
\displaystyle{\mathbb{T}_{\psi,\varphi}}
is called the transition (scattering) amplitude from the state
\mid\varphi>
to a final state
\mid\psi>
. We can consult the following references on the subject which summarizes the actual knowledge of Reggeon theory (
[Gribov2]
,
[Baker et al.]
,
[Kaidalov]
and
[Boreskov et al.]
).

6
Generalized diagonalisation of semigroup
\displaystyle{e^{-tH_{\lambda^{\prime\prime},\lambda^{\prime},\mu,\lambda}}}
,
t>0

6.1
Diagonalization of semigroup
e^{-tH_{\lambda^{\prime}}}
,
t>0
where
H_{\lambda^{\prime}}=\lambda^{\prime}A^{*^{2}}A^{2}+\mu A^{*}A+i\lambda(A^{*}(A+A^{*})A,\,\lambda^{\prime}>0

In this under-section, we consider the following Cauchy problem :

u^{\prime}(t)=H_{\lambda^{\prime}}u(t);u(0)=\phi\in B

(6.1)

Let
\{\phi_{k}\};k=1,2,....
are the eigenfunctions of
H_{\lambda^{\prime}}
associated to eigenvalues
\{\sigma_{k};k=1,2,....\}
.

what conditions can be imposed on
H_{\lambda^{\prime}}
to write
u(t)
as a limit of partial sums of expression
\displaystyle{\sum_{k=1}^{+\infty}C_{k}e^{-\sigma_{k}t}\phi_{k}}
?

Let
\lambda^{\prime}\neq 0
, we will apply a fine result of Lidskii
[Lidskii]
to show the existence of a total system of eigenfunctions of
H_{\lambda^{\prime}}
.

Proposition 6.1
.

(
[Intissar2]
)

Let
\lambda^{\prime}>0
and
\lambda^{\prime 2}\leq\mu\lambda^{\prime}+\lambda^{2}
, we consider the Cauchy’s problem :

u^{\prime}(t)=H_{\lambda^{\prime}}u(t);t>0
and
u(0)=\phi\in B

\displaystyle{H_{\lambda^{\prime}}=(\lambda^{\prime}z^{2}+i\lambda z)\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial z^{2}}+(i\lambda z^{2}+\mu z)\frac{\partial}{\partial z}}
acting on Bargmann space

\displaystyle{\mathbb{B}=\{\varphi:\mathbb{C}\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}\,entire\,;\int_{\mathbb{C}}\mid\varphi(z)\mid^{2}e^{-\mid z\mid^{2}}dxdy<+\infty\}}

Then we have
u(t)=\displaystyle{\sum_{k=1}^{+\infty}\frac{<\phi,\phi_{k}^{*}>}{<\phi_{k},\phi_{k}^{*}>}e^{-\sigma_{k}t}\phi_{k}}
where

\phi_{k}\in D(H_{\lambda^{\prime}})
and
\phi_{k}^{*}\in D(H_{\lambda^{\prime}}^{*})=D(H_{\lambda^{\prime}})=D(S)

H_{\lambda^{\prime}}\phi_{k}=\sigma_{k}\phi_{k}
et
H_{\lambda^{\prime}}^{*}\phi_{k}^{*}=\sigma_{k}\phi_{k}^{*}

The proof of this proposition is based on a thin Lidskii theorem which will be given below in simplified form.

Theorem 6.2
.

(
[Lidskii]
, theorem 3, p:208)

Let
T
be a linear operator with dense domain
D(T)
in Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
compact resolvent.

We suppose that the eigenvalues of
T
are simple and
0\in\rho(T)
(
\rho(T)
is the resolvent set. of
T
).

Let

\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}u^{\prime}(t)+Tu(t)=0;t>0\\
\\
and\\
\\
u(0)=\phi\in\mathbb{B}\quad\quad\quad\quad.\\
\end{array}\right.

(6.2)

If
T
satisfies the following two conditions

(i)
\exists\quad p;0<p<1
and
\displaystyle{\sum_{k=1}^{+\infty}\mid\alpha_{k}\mid^{p}}
converge

where
\{\alpha_{k}\};k=1,2,.....
are the eigenvalues of
\sqrt{T^{*^{-1}}T^{-1}}
.

(ii)
\exists\quad\epsilon>0;-\frac{\pi}{2}+\epsilon\leq<arg<T\phi,\phi>\leq\frac{\pi}{2}-\epsilon\quad\forall\phi\in D(T)

Then

(1) The problem (6.2) admits an unique solution.

(2) The solution
u(t)
of Cauchy’s problem (6.2) is a limit of partial sums of expression

\displaystyle{\sum_{k=1}^{+\infty}\frac{<\phi,\phi_{k}^{*}>}{<\phi_{k},\phi_{k}^{*}>}e^{-\beta_{k}t}\phi_{k}}
o
\grave{u}

\phi_{k}\in D(T)
and
\phi_{k}^{*}\in D(T^{*})

and

T\phi_{k}=\beta_{k}\phi_{k}
and
T^{*}\phi_{k}^{*}=\overline{\beta}_{k}\phi_{k}^{*}

Let
\beta_{0}>0
and
H_{\lambda^{\prime}}=H_{1}+iH_{2}
where
